## Preface

Every system worth building eventually leaves the hands of the people who designed it. It enters a world they cannot fully foresee, a world of real users, real traffic and real failures, which arrive at three in the morning and on the busiest day of the year. We call that world *production*. For most of our field's history we treated production as the place software went after the interesting work was done: engineers wrote the code, and somebody else, usually in another department and often on another floor, kept it running. That division never served anyone well. The people who understood the system were not the people woken by it, and the people woken by it could rarely change it. Over the last fifteen years the division has quietly dissolved, and the tools for running software have been remade along the way. Dashboards gave way to traces and wide events, pagers to incident platforms, runbooks to self-service workflows, and most recently AI assistants have begun to read our telemetry and sit in our incident channels.

This book is about the part of that work which the tools do not do for us. Its thesis is that production is a sociotechnical system: its reliability is produced, minute by minute, by people and machines together, and so it cannot be understood by studying either one alone. A well-engineered system still has to be run. Someone has to decide what to automate and what to keep in human hands, someone has to see what the system is actually doing, someone has to take command when it fails in a way nobody anticipated, and someone has to make sure that the organization learns from the failure instead of merely recovering from it. The book takes those kinds of work in turn: how people and machines *run* a system together, how they *see* it, how they *respond* when it fails, and how they *learn* afterwards.

None of the lessons beneath that work was learned in software first. A system of many interacting parts fails in ways that no single part explains, because the failure lives in the interactions. Automation takes away the easy work and leaves people the hard work, and it tends to hand that hard work back at the worst possible moment. Organizations drift toward the edge of what they can safely do, not through any single bad decision but one reasonable exception at a time. These things were learned, at great cost, in coal mines, nuclear plants, cockpits, operating theaters and launch pads, and in the wildfires that taught a generation of firefighters how to command a response among strangers. I have come to believe that the newest operational practices are best understood, and best used, by someone who knows the older lessons that explain why those practices work. A team that adds an operations agent without knowing what automation does to the people beside it, or that writes blameless postmortems without understanding drift, has the tool without the reason for it, and it will misuse the tool the first time the situation departs from the tutorial.

This book is written for the reader who wants both. That reader might be an engineer who builds services and now also runs them, a team lead who has inherited an on-call rotation along with a team, or an architect who wants to know what becomes of a design once it meets the world. AI appears where it really changes the practice (the operations agent of Chapter 3, the assistant that queries telemetry in Chapter 4, the incident channel of Chapter 5) and nowhere else, because a book that put AI into every chapter would be teaching fashion. The tools will keep changing. The judgment they require will not.

The book belongs to a series that asks one question from several sides. *Software Architecture in the AI & Cloud Era*, which these pages call the Architecture book{% include xref.html to="architecture" %}, asks how to shape a system. *Building Resilient Systems in the AI & Cloud Era*, the Resilience book{% include xref.html to="resilience" %}, asks how to engineer that system for production: its objectives, capacity, runtime, delivery, failure handling, data, security and AI workloads. This book, its companion, asks how to operate it: how the people and machines who run the system keep it running, see into it, fight its failures and learn from them. One book is about the system; the other is about the people and machines that keep it alive. The two share a company, a cast and a single bad morning, and each can be read on its own.

### Influences & Attribution

Much of this book's method comes from others, and I want to be plain about where. Each chapter's references point to the original works, which are always the better read; this book's job is to connect them and to show them working together at one company. Its intellectual foundation is safety science, and it comes from outside software entirely. Charles Perrow, James Reason, Jens Rasmussen, Richard Cook, David Woods, Erik Hollnagel, Sidney Dekker, Nancy Leveson and Diane Vaughan studied how complex systems fail and how people keep them working, and Lisanne Bainbridge, Thomas Sheridan, Raja Parasuraman, John Lee and Gary Klein studied what automation does to the people who work beside it. They wrote about aviation, medicine, nuclear power and spaceflight, fields where failure kills people and where the study of failure was therefore taken seriously decades before ours. Their work turns out to describe software operations with uncomfortable accuracy. Reading them is a little like finding a detailed map of a country you thought you were the first to explore.

The practice of running software draws on Google's *Site Reliability Engineering* and *The Site Reliability Workbook*, and on James Hamilton's advice on operating internet-scale services. Observability draws on Charity Majors, Liz Fong-Jones and George Miranda's *Observability Engineering*, on the work of Cindy Sridharan and Yuri Shkuro, and on the OpenTelemetry project. Incident response follows the Incident Command System as Rob Schnepp, Ron Vidal and Chris Hawley carried it into software operations, together with PagerDuty's published response process, Mica Endsley's work on situation awareness and Karl Weick's on sensemaking. Learning from incidents draws on John Allspaw, on Dekker's just culture, on Leveson's CAST, on the STELLA report and on the learning-from-incidents community gathered around the VOID. The organization chapter draws on Ron Westrum, Amy Edmondson, Matthew Skelton and Manuel Pais's *Team Topologies*, and the DORA research of Nicole Forsgren and colleagues.

Several terms used in this book as common vocabulary were coined by specific people or groups, and they deserve the credit: toil and the Wheel of Misfortune (Google SRE), the USE method (Brendan Gregg), the RED method (Tom Wilkie), gray failure (Peng Huang and colleagues), the core analysis loop (Majors, Fong-Jones and Miranda), the do-nothing script (Dan Slimmon), normalization of deviance (Diane Vaughan), Safety-I and Safety-II (Erik Hollnagel), STAMP, STPA and CAST (Nancy Leveson), the Swiss cheese model (James Reason), situation awareness (Mica Endsley), psychological safety (Amy Edmondson) and the typology of organizational cultures (Ron Westrum).

### Authorship & Disclaimer

This book was written and directed by Oleksiy Pylypenko, with AI assistance (Anthropic's Claude). The judgment in it comes from twenty-plus years of practice: platform engineering inside a large bank, API architecture in fintech, and a decade of maintaining a widely used open-source library. The author chose the thesis, the structure, the examples and the trade-offs, edited every chapter, and takes responsibility for every claim, errors included. Encore and every kata company are fictional, and historical events are described from the public record. The content is provided as-is, without warranty of any kind. It is educational material, not professional advice for any specific system, and the license's full disclaimer of warranties applies.

## How to Read This Book

One company runs through all seven chapters. **Encore** sells tickets to live concerts, and its traffic can rise 500× in minutes when a major tour is announced, which makes it a demanding place to learn how to run software. Readers of the Architecture book watched Encore grow from nine engineers to 400, and readers of the Resilience book watched it engineer its system for production. This book asks what it takes to operate that system: who runs it, how they see it, and what they do when it fails. Chapter 1 introduces Encore for readers meeting it for the first time, each later chapter adds the parts of the company it needs, and an appendix gathers all of them on one page for reference.

One incident sits at the center of the book: the on-sale for a fictional artist named Vesper Hale. The Resilience book ends on the morning of that on-sale, with Encore's preparations complete and the Gate about to open. This book takes the morning from 10:00, fights it live in Chapter 5 and learns from it in Chapter 6, and the chapters before them point out, one at a time, the small and reasonable decisions that will matter when it comes. Either book can be read first. A reader who starts here meets the incident as the people in the channel did, without knowing everything that led to it; a reader who starts with the Resilience book arrives at 10:00 knowing the system's weak points rather better than its operators did.

Each chapter closes with a **kata**, a practice brief for a different company with its own scale and constraints, together with a rubric to check your work against. The word comes from the martial arts, where a kata is a set form practiced until it becomes second nature, and the idea is much the same as a musician's scales: the value lies in repetition in a place where mistakes are cheap, so that the moves are familiar when mistakes become expensive. Every section ends with a short recap and an exercise. The exercises work best done against a system you actually run, because the point is to see the idea in your own production rather than in Encore's. Each chapter's references list the field's standard works, and those marked *free online* cost nothing to read.

The chapters build on one another, but each can be read on its own. A reader in the middle of an on-call crisis may reasonably start with Chapter 5, and a reader whose dashboards are green while users complain may start with Chapter 4. Chapters 2 and 3 are different. They lay the foundation the rest of the book stands on, the ideas about failure and automation that explain why every later practice takes the shape it does, and I would ask every reader to make time for them eventually.

## The Arc

The seven chapters fall into four parts. They move from understanding what production is and how it fails, through seeing a running system, to responding when it fails and learning afterwards, and they end with the organization that does all of this.

**Foundations (Chapters 1–3):** what production is, how complex systems fail, and what automation does to the people who run it.

**Seeing the system (Chapter 4):** observability, from the failures we expected to the questions nobody thought to ask in advance.
{: .flush}

**Responding and learning (Chapters 5–6):** incident response on the morning of the Vesper Hale on-sale, and learning from it afterwards.
{: .flush}

**The organization (Chapter 7):** the teams, skills and culture that run production, ending with the capstone.
{: .flush}

## Chapter 1: What Production Is — Running Software in the Cloud Era {#ch1}

> Code is finished when it ships. Production never is.

On the morning after Encore's new Notification service went live, nothing happened, and that quiet was the first thing worth noticing. The Notification service has a modest job: it delivers each ticket a fan has bought to the fan's phone wallet and email inbox. Overnight a coding agent had rebuilt it on a new framework, with better retry semantics and 40 files that no person had typed or read in full. Every automated safeguard Encore had built then did its work. The contract tests, which check that a service still keeps the promises other services depend on, passed. The boundary rules, which check that the code respects the lines the architecture draws between services, passed. The security checks passed. Last came the canary, a release in which the new version first serves a small share of real traffic while the old one serves the rest. The canary was judged against the service's **service level objective (SLO)**, the stated target for how well it must serve its users, and because it met that target it was promoted to all traffic. By breakfast the service had delivered about 180,000 tickets, and every dashboard was green.

One engineer had nonetheless spent 20 minutes on that change before it merged. She read the behavior it promised more closely than the code itself, and she found that the new retry semantics could, under the wrong timing, send a fan the same ticket twice. Retrying is a sound instinct for a service like this one, because a mail server or wallet provider that does not answer the first time will often answer the second. The hazard of every retry is that the first attempt may have succeeded after all, only slowly, so that the second attempt delivers a second copy. No automated check would have noticed, because from the point of view of every check Encore had, a duplicate ticket is a perfectly healthy delivery that happened twice. She added a specification-level idempotency test, one that states the rule in the terms of the specification rather than the code (a purchased ticket reaches the fan once, however many times delivery is attempted), and the change went out.

The dashboards that morning were honest; they simply could not see the one thing that mattered, and a person had seen it for them. That is why the green screens raise this book's question, a question older than it looks. Who runs code that nobody fully read, and what do they need from it at 3 a.m., when the engineer on call is tired, the author of the code (human or agent) is nowhere near, and the system is doing something that no test anticipated?

The coding agent makes the question vivid, but it did not invent it. People have always run code they did not write, and learning to run it well without understanding all of it is the founding question of our discipline. I have come to believe that it remains the right question, and that the agent has only removed our last excuse for pretending otherwise. This chapter traces how that question was answered over time, from the machine-room operators of the 1950s to Google's first site reliability engineering (SRE) team in 2003. It then sets out three things in turn: what users actually feel of production, the stack of practices we use to run it, and the principles that make a system operable by strangers, meaning people who did not build it and cannot hold all of it in their heads. On a system the size of Encore's, that describes everyone.

### Encore, the System We Will Run {#encore}

Encore is our companion again, as it was throughout [the Architecture book{% include xref.html to="architecture" %}](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#encore-our-running-example), which followed the company from nine engineers to 400. For most of the week Encore could pass for an ordinary online shop that happens to sell seats at concerts across Europe, priced in euros. Then a famous artist's tour goes on sale, nearly always at 10:00 on a Friday, and within minutes the traffic reaches 500 times its weekday level. Three demands arrive together on that morning. The first is scale: a million people want a few thousand seats, and most of them will be disappointed. The second is correctness: every seat must be sold exactly once, which means it must never be lost and never be sold twice to two fans who clicked it in the same instant. The third is adversarial: the bots arrive before the fans do, because a reseller's scripts are faster and more numerous than any person with a phone. Nearly every structure in Encore exists to answer one of those three demands, and most of the trouble in this book lies where they pull against each other, as when a defense against bots must not turn away the very fans it exists to protect.

On such a morning the first problem is the size of the crowd, and only after that the selling of seats, so Encore is built to manage the crowd first. A concert hall solves the same problem with doors and ushers: nobody lets ten thousand people sprint for the seats at once, because the aisles would jam and the people at the front would be crushed. Figure 1.1 is the map of how Encore does the equivalent, and the part of it that matters most is short enough to walk through now. The fan's **purchase path**, the chain of services a fan passes through between arriving and holding a ticket, begins at the **Sale Gate**, Encore's virtual waiting room. There fans meet a queue instead of a seat map and are let through at a metered rate of about 1,200 fans a second per event. That rate is chosen so that nothing behind the Gate runs hot; it keeps the load on Seat Inventory below 80% of what Seat Inventory can carry, leaving room for the unexpected. This makes the Gate an **admission controller**: a component that protects a scarce resource by deciding how much work may reach it. Its rate is the most powerful single control Encore has, because every service behind it sees only the load the Gate lets through, and that is why a change to the rate deserves far more care than its one number suggests. The usher analogy holds well up to a point and then breaks in an instructive way. An usher can see the hall filling and slow the line by eye, whereas the Gate cannot see the services behind it at all; its rate is a judgment made in advance, from capacity arithmetic and load tests, and it is only as good as the conditions under which that judgment was made.

Alongside the Gate, **Bot Screening** judges, session by session, whether it faces a fan or a script, and it must do so quickly and without a person in the loop, since a million sessions cannot be reviewed by hand. Past the Gate and the screening, **Seat Inventory** is the single authority on whether a seat is free. When a fan picks seats, Seat Inventory holds them for five minutes so that nobody else can take them while the fan pays, and it sells each seat once, whatever else in the system is failing. Every other service may slow down, degrade or refuse work, but Seat Inventory may not get this one thing wrong, which is why it is built more conservatively than anything around it. **Orders & Payments** then turns the hold into a sale within a 3 s checkout budget, calling an external payment service provider (PSP), with a second provider ready to take new payments if the first one falters. That second provider was not always there. The PSP's slowness once took the whole site down, when a dependency outside Encore's control slowed and the services waiting on it exhausted themselves; [the Architecture book](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#anatomy-of-a-self-inflicted-outage) told that story, and Chapter 2 reads it again through the lens of safety science.

{% include diagrams/fig-1-1.svg %}

*Figure 1.1 — Encore's system map. The purchase path runs down the middle; everything else either hangs off it or listens to it.*

A map shows what exists, but it does not show the order in which a fan meets it. Figure 1.2 follows one fan along the purchase path, from the waiting room to a ticket in the phone wallet, so that the services above appear in the sequence a person actually experiences them.

{% include diagrams/fig-1-2.svg %}

*Figure 1.2 — One fan's purchase in eight steps, each tagged with the service that owns it. The admission rate and the checkout budget bound the rush from opposite ends.*

Read the figure from both ends and a useful symmetry appears. At the entrance, the admission rate limits how many fans begin the journey each second; at the exit, the checkout budget limits how long any one fan may take to finish it. Between those two bounds lies everything Encore must keep healthy during an on-sale, and a surprising number of the failures in later chapters come from something in the middle quietly violating an assumption that one of the two bounds depended on.

Software this size is not run by one heroic group, and Encore's arrangement reflects the lessons of the Architecture book. Forty teams build these services and carry their own pagers, which is to say that the team that writes a service is also the team woken when it misbehaves. They stand on a platform whose golden paths, the supported and well-lit routes through the platform's tools, take a new service from template to production without each team rediscovering how. Figure 1.3 shows where all of it runs. Within the platform group sits the **Reliability team**, ten engineers who own the tooling for service level objectives, the incident process and the readiness review held before every big on-sale. They advise, embed with teams for large launches and rehearse failures with them, but they do not run other teams' services. The distinction matters more than any org chart can show: a reliability team that runs everyone's services soon becomes the only group that understands them, whereas one that teaches and rehearses leaves the understanding where the pagers are.

{% include diagrams/fig-1-3.svg %}

*Figure 1.3 — Where Encore runs: two EU regions of Kubernetes cells and serverless edges, reconciled from one Git repository. The seat ledger commits each write to a majority of five replicas in three locations, two in each region and a witness in a third, which is why losing a region loses no sale.*

The figure's caption compresses an important idea that later chapters unpack. Because a seat sale counts as committed only once a majority of the five replicas has recorded it, no single location holds the only copy of the truth, and the loss of an entire region leaves enough replicas elsewhere to carry on without forgetting a sale. The Resilience book's chapter on data and disaster recovery explains how that works and what it costs; for now it is enough to know that Seat Inventory's promise of selling each seat exactly once is backed by more than good intentions.

One Friday runs through this book: the on-sale for Vesper Hale, a fictional artist whose 12-date European tour will bring about 1.1 million fans to the Gate in the same minute. The Resilience book ends on the morning of that on-sale, with Encore's preparations done and the Gate about to open; this book takes the morning from 10:00. Chapter 5 fights that morning as it happens, and Chapter 6 learns from it afterwards. The chapters before them point out, one at a time, small and reasonable decisions that will matter on that morning, each defensible on its own day and by its own owners, which is how serious incidents in real systems are usually assembled. Everything else in Encore, from its tenants and cells to the change freeze and the assistant in the incident channel, is introduced in the chapter where it first does something, so that no piece arrives before the reader has a reason to care about it. For the reader who wants the whole company on one page, [the appendix](/prodsys-book/#encore-glance) keeps a glossary.

### 1.1 Production as a Sociotechnical System

#### What we mean by production {#what-we-mean-by-production}

Before we can ask who runs code that nobody fully read, we should be clear about where that code runs. The word *production* comes to us from manufacturing, where it names the floor on which real goods are made, as distinct from the laboratory where they are designed and tried. In a laboratory a failed experiment is information; on the factory floor a failed batch is a loss. Software borrowed the word because it needed the same distinction. **Production** is the environment in which software serves real users with real consequences: where a mistake costs money, trust or someone's evening, and where nothing can be undone merely by closing a laptop. A bug on a developer's machine is a puzzle to be solved at leisure. The same bug in production is an event that is already happening to someone, and it keeps happening until a person or a machine intervenes.

The factory, though, is not the most useful picture of what production is like to run, and the theatre teaches something the factory does not. A play's script is written once, but the production is made again every night. Each performance is assembled live by a crew that adjusts to a cold house, a sticking piece of scenery or a missed cue, and a good stage manager keeps the show running through small failures the audience never sees. That is much closer to running software than the image of a finished product rolling off a line, because a running service is never finished either: it is performed, continuously, by the code together with the people who watch over it. Encore's Notification service did not stop needing attention once the canary promoted it; it began needing a different kind.

The analogy breaks at scale, and the break tells us something. A stage crew can see the whole stage, and when an actor misses a mark, someone in the wings notices within seconds. Encore runs thousands of copies of its services for millions of people it never sees, and no one at Encore can look at that stage. Everything its engineers know about what the system is doing, they know indirectly, through measurements, logs and the occasional complaint. Between what is actually happening and what anyone can perceive there is always a gap, sometimes small and sometimes wide enough to hide a duplicate ticket. Most of the techniques in this book live in that gap: they exist either to narrow it or to let people act sensibly in spite of it.

#### People and machines, jointly {#people-and-machines-jointly}

The idea that a working system is made of people and machines together, and must be understood that way, is older than computing. The term *sociotechnical system* comes from the Tavistock Institute in London, where Eric Trist and Ken Bamforth studied British coal mines in work published in 1951. The mines had adopted the longwall method, which on paper was technically superior: it replaced small-scale digging with a long coal face worked mechanically along its length. Yet the mines had become worse. The old method had been organized around small, self-managing teams whose members could each do several jobs and who looked out for one another underground, and those teams were what had kept the work safe. The longwall method broke them apart into a sequence of narrow, specialized shifts, each dependent on the one before and none responsible for the whole. Better machinery, installed into a worse social arrangement, produced a worse mine. Trist and Bamforth's conclusion, which nearly every industry has had to relearn since, was that the social and the technical parts of a work system cannot be optimized separately. Improve one in isolation and you may well damage the system you meant to improve.

With that history behind us, we can state the definition plainly. A **sociotechnical system** is one whose behavior is produced jointly by its people and its technology, so that neither can be understood or improved without the other. Production is such a system, and its reliability is no more a property of the code than a ship's safety is a property of its hull. A sound hull matters a great deal, but a ship is kept safe by its crew's watchkeeping, its charts, its maintenance and the judgment of whoever is on the bridge, and the same hull can come home or be lost depending on all of these. In the same way, a reliable service is not something that was built once and now sits there being reliable. Its reliability is produced continuously, by five kinds of work: people *knowing* what the system is doing, *changing* it carefully, *withstanding* what goes wrong, *responding* when it does, and *learning* afterwards. Those five verbs organize this book and its companion, the Resilience book{% include xref.html to="resilience" %}: that book takes up changing and withstanding, the engineering of the system itself, while this one takes up knowing, responding and learning, together with the automation and the organization that run beneath all five. In my experience a team which neglects any one of them will sooner or later find that the other four cannot make up for it.

The Notification rewrite from this chapter's opening shows what the definition means in practice. Judged as an artifact, the new service was better than the old one: a newer framework and better retry semantics. Judged as part of a sociotechnical system, it was briefly worse, because its operators understood less of it. Forty files had arrived that no person had read in full, and knowledge of how those files behaved under awkward timing existed in none of Encore's people. The engineer who spent 20 minutes on the change was doing sociotechnical work in the plainest sense. Finding the duplicate-ticket case was half of it; the other half was what she did next, which was more durable than remembering the case: she wrote it down as the specification-level idempotency test we met in the opening. That test moved a piece of human knowledge into a check that would outlast her attention, so that the next change, whether made by her, by a colleague or by another agent, would be held to the same promise. Turning what one person noticed into something the system enforces is the oldest move in operations, and Section 1.2 follows it back to the machine room.

One more distinction is worth carrying forward, and it comes from [the Architecture book](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#12-architectural-characteristics): the difference between complexity that is essential to a problem and complexity that we add by the way we solve it. In that language, Encore's 500× spike on a Friday morning is essential complexity. It comes from the business itself, from a million fans wanting a few thousand seats at the same moment, and no amount of good engineering will make it go away; the best we can do is meet it. The gap between what a system does and what its operators perceive is different in kind. It is usually accidental, the by-product of dashboards that measure the wrong thing, behavior no one wrote down and changes no one fully read. Accidental complexity can be reduced, and much of operations is the patient work of closing that gap, one test, alert or runbook at a time.

**Recap.** Production is where software meets real consequences, and it is better pictured as a performance renewed every night than as a finished product. It is a sociotechnical system: its reliability is produced jointly by people and machines, through knowing, changing, withstanding, responding and learning. Much operational work is the closing of an accidental gap, the one between what the system does and what its people can perceive.

**Exercise 1.1.** List the last five times a person, rather than an automated check, prevented or shortened a problem with a service you know. For each one, ask whether that person's knowledge now lives in a test, an alert or a runbook, or whether it still lives only in their head. Where it lives only in a head, write down what it would take to move it into the system.

### 1.2 From Operators to SRE — A Short History of Running Software

#### The machine room {#the-machine-room}

In September 1947 someone working on the Harvard Mark II taped a moth into the machine's logbook, beside the note "First actual case of bug being found." The moth is the part of the story everyone remembers, but the logbook is the more instructive object. Someone had thought it worth keeping a written record of what the machine did, when it misbehaved and what was done about it, so that the next person on shift could know what the last one had seen. Running a computer has always meant recording what went wrong, and that habit is older than almost anything else in our trade. The incident timelines of Chapter 5, with their minute-by-minute entries, continue the same record on the same principle.

Through the 1950s and 1960s, as machines such as the IBM 704 and later the System/360 spread through universities, banks and government, looking after them became a job in its own right. The **computer operator** was the person who kept a machine running and fed it work, and the job came with its own shifts and skills. Most installations ran as a *closed shop*, which is to say that programmers did not touch the machine at all. They handed their programs, punched onto decks of cards, across a counter, and came back hours later to collect the printout. Behind the counter, operators did everything else: they mounted the tapes each job needed, watched the console for signs of trouble, and restarted jobs that had failed. To restart a job they followed instructions the programmer had left, often kept in a binder called a run book. The word has survived, barely changed, in the runbooks Encore's teams keep today.

The operator's position deserves a closer look, because it is the position Encore's on-call engineer stood in on the morning after the Notification rewrite. Operators ran programs they had not written and could not usefully read; there was neither the time nor, usually, the training to work out what a deck of cards was meant to do. What made the arrangement workable was a kind of contract between the authors and the operators, in three parts. The program declared what it needed. The run book said what to do when it stopped. And the operator's judgment covered whatever the first two had not anticipated. When the contract failed, it tended to fail at one of those three parts. The instructions had gone stale, because the program had changed and the binder had not. The program had been written for the happy path and had nothing sensible to say when anything else happened. Or the operator was left improvising at 3 a.m. while the people who understood the program slept. Each of those failures has a modern form, and a good deal of this book is about preventing them.

#### Automating the operator {#automating-the-operator}

Seen from far enough away, much of computing's history is the operator's work being absorbed, piece by piece, into software. One of the first pieces went early. In 1956 General Motors Research and North American Aviation wrote GM-NAA I/O for the IBM 704, one of the first operating systems, and their chief aim was to cut the idle time between jobs, the minutes in which an expensive machine waited for a person to load the next one. Moving from one job to the next had been the operator's task; now a program did it. Spooling and time-sharing absorbed more of the role in the years that followed. By the 1980s the operator's work had passed to the **system administrator**, who tended fleets of smaller Unix machines rather than one large one, and who did much of that tending through scripts. The scripts are the telling detail. The administrator was no longer only doing the work but writing it down in a form a machine could carry out unattended, which is the same move, made in code, that the run book had made on paper.

Aviation went through a parallel change, and setting the two side by side shows what kind of change it was. Large airliners once carried a third crew member, the flight engineer, who managed the engines, fuel and electrical systems from a panel of their own. From the early 1980s new wide-body airliners, beginning with the Boeing 767 and the Airbus A310, were certified to fly with two pilots, and the flight engineer's panel was folded into automation. The role did not so much vanish as dissolve. Part of it went into the aircraft's design, which now watched over its own systems, and part went into the pilots' training, which now covered what the engineer used to know. Computer operations dissolved in the same way: into operating systems, then into scripts, and eventually into the code itself, as services learned to restart, scale and report on themselves.

This analogy, too, has a limit worth marking. An airliner type is certified once and then flown with its design largely settled, so the automation that replaced the flight engineer could be examined thoroughly before anyone depended on it. Software changes daily, and the automation that takes over an operator's task is itself changed, redeployed and occasionally broken along with everything else. We never get to certify our automation once and stop worrying about it. Yet both histories share one constant. Every era of automation absorbed the tasks that could be written down, and every era left behind the thing that could not be: judgment for the failure nobody had written down. That remainder is still the operator's job, and it is the part of the job this book cares about most.

#### The wall, and two ways over it {#the-wall-and-two-ways-over-it}

As software moved onto the web, the problem changed shape, from a technical one to an organizational one. Developers were rewarded for change, for shipping features, while operations staff were rewarded for stability, for keeping the site up. Each group behaved sensibly given what it was measured on, and the result was a wall between them, over which developers threw finished releases for operations to deal with. The IT Infrastructure Library (ITIL), a body of service-management guidance published by the UK government from the late 1980s, brought real discipline to how changes were proposed, approved and recorded. But it often institutionalized the wall, turning the hand-off from one group to the other into a formal process rather than questioning why the hand-off existed.

Two answers emerged. The first came from Google. In 2003 Benjamin Treynor (now Ben Treynor Sloss) joined the company to run a production team of seven engineers, and he staffed it with software engineers who treated operations as a software problem. That choice led to three working rules. Repetitive operational work was to be automated, as any software engineer would want to automate it. The manual share of each engineer's time was capped at half, so that the other half went into the engineering that reduced the manual work. And the balance between change and stability, which the wall had left to argument, was to be set by measuring it, so that the two sides could agree on a number instead of a mood. Treynor later put the idea in a line that has been quoted ever since: "SRE is what happens when you ask a software engineer to design an operations team."

The other answer came from the web companies. In 2009 John Allspaw and Paul Hammond described Flickr's practice of deploying more than 10 times a day, made possible by developers and operations engineers working together, and later that year Patrick Debois organized the first DevOpsDays in Ghent. Where SRE built a better kind of operations team, DevOps tried to take down the wall itself. Amazon's "you build it, you run it", which [the Architecture book discussed](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#115-operability-by-design), went furthest: it pushed the pager to the team that wrote the code, so that the people who made a change were the people woken when it failed.

Encore has inherited all three of these ideas, as most organizations of its size have. Its teams run their own services, in the spirit of "you build it, you run it". A small Reliability team works in the SRE tradition, owning the tooling and practices that help every other team measure and protect its reliability. And the incident commander rotation borrows trained engineers, most of them senior, from everywhere, so that a serious incident is led by someone practiced at leading incidents. Beneath all three, the question is still the one the closed shop faced across its counter: how do we run software whose authors are not in the room? Until recently the authors were at least somewhere, asleep or busy on another project, and could in principle be woken and asked. With a coding agent as the author, as with the Notification rewrite, they may not be anywhere. Figure 1.4 sets the lineage on one line.

{% include diagrams/fig-1-4.svg %}

*Figure 1.4 — Who has stood between the code and its users, from the 1947 logbook to today.*

**Recap.** Operators have always run code they did not write. They have relied on a contract with its authors, made of declared needs and written instructions, and on their own judgment for the cases nobody wrote down. Each era of automation has absorbed more of the written part, and none has removed the need for judgment. SRE and DevOps were two answers to the wall between those rewarded for change and those rewarded for stability, one building a better operations team and the other dissolving the boundary, and today they are usually combined, as they are at Encore.

**Exercise 1.2.** Draw your organization's closed-shop counter, the point where code passes from those who wrote it to those who run it. Who stands on each side? What crosses the counter with the code: tests, dashboards, runbooks, a conversation? And what is lost in the crossing, the knowledge that stays with the authors but that the people running the code will need at 3 a.m.?

### 1.3 Reliability, Availability, and What Users Actually Feel

#### Definitions worth quoting {#definitions-worth-quoting}

Before we can say whether Encore is running well, we need words precise enough to argue with, and the best of them are older than the web. They come from the reliability engineers of the 1950s, who faced military electronics that failed at alarming rates and needed a way to say, in numbers, how much better a radio or a guidance system had to become. The definition they settled on has held up remarkably well: **reliability** is the probability that a system performs its intended function, under stated conditions, for a stated period.

Each of the three qualifying phrases in that definition does real work, and each is worth a moment. *Intended function* means that a system can be running and still be failing: a seat map that loads quickly but shows the wrong seats as free is up, and it is not doing its job. *Stated conditions* means that reliability is always reliability under some load and some circumstances, so the fact that Encore behaves beautifully on a quiet Tuesday tells us nothing about how it behaves during an on-sale, when the traffic is 500 times greater. *Stated period* means that reliability describes a window of time, never a single instant; a system is reliable over a month or over an on-sale hour, and the question "is it reliable right now?" has no answer.

**Availability** is a narrower idea: the fraction of time, or of requests, for which the system is able to serve. Its classical steady-state form comes from the same engineering tradition and is easy to state. Take the **mean time between failures (MTBF)**, the average stretch of good service between one failure and the next, and the **mean time to repair (MTTR)**, the average time it takes to restore service once a failure has happened. Availability is the share of each cycle spent working, which is MTBF divided by the sum of MTBF and MTTR.

The formula looks innocent, but it carries a design lesson. Availability can rise in two ways: by failing less often, which lengthens the time between failures, or by recovering faster, which shortens the time to repair. A short worked example shows that the two levers are equally strong. Suppose a component fails on average once every 1,000 hours and takes an hour to restore; it is available 1,000 hours out of every 1,001, or about 99.9% of the time. Doubling the time between failures to 2,000 hours, with the same hour of repair, gives about 99.95%. Halving the repair time to half an hour, with failures still every 1,000 hours, gives the same 99.95%. On paper the two improvements are worth exactly the same.

In practice they rarely cost the same. Modern systems are built from many parts, from containers and network links to managed services and other teams' APIs, and those parts fail partially and often. Making every one of them fail half as often is expensive and frequently impossible, because many of them belong to someone else. Making the whole system notice a failure and route around it in half the time is usually far cheaper, and it helps no matter which part failed. That is why "expect failures" was the first tenet of James Hamilton's 2007 paper on internet-scale services: once we accept that parts will fail, the engineering effort moves from preventing every failure to recovering from each one quickly. Figure 1.5 draws the arithmetic.

{% include diagrams/fig-1-5.svg %}

*Figure 1.5 — Availability as mean time between failures over the sum of that and mean time to repair, with lines of equal availability. Halving recovery time raises availability as much as doubling the time between failures.* The Resilience book's first chapter turns availability into targets.

There is a trap here, and many organizations have walked into it. The lesson of Figure 1.5 concerns design: build the system so that it can recover quickly. It does not follow that average recovery time is a good number to manage a team by, because real incidents are too few, and their durations too wildly skewed, for an average to mean much. A quarter might contain six incidents, five of them resolved in minutes and one that ran for hours, and that one long incident decides the average almost on its own. Štěpán Davidovič showed by simulation at Google that even a genuine 10% improvement in incident durations is usually lost in the noise of MTTR, so a team that really did get better would often see no change in the number, and a team that got no better might see the number improve by luck. The VOID, Courtney Nash's public collection of thousands of incident reports, shows the same skew across many organizations. So this book judges recovery in other ways: by drills, where we can rehearse recovery under conditions we choose, and by what each real incident teaches us, which is the subject of Chapter 6.

Reliability and availability are not the whole story, and for Encore they are not even the most important part of it. A third property matters most. **Correctness** is the property that the system's answers are right: each seat is sold once, every fan who was charged gets a ticket, and each ticket is delivered once. The dependability literature does not ignore correctness; it folds it into the definitions themselves, so that Avižienis and colleagues' 2004 taxonomy defines availability as readiness for *correct* service. But availability as we usually measure it counts responses. It records whether the system answered, and says nothing about whether the answer was right, while users judge exactly the thing it leaves out: what the system said.

The Notification rewrite from this chapter's opening shows how wide that gap can be. Had the engineer not caught the retry bug before it merged, the duplicate ticket would have appeared nowhere in Encore's availability measure, because every duplicate was a successful delivery. The dashboard would have stayed green while fans opened their wallets and found two copies of one seat, and wondered which one would get them through the door.

#### Vital signs and the patient {#vital-signs-and-the-patient}

If availability can be perfect while fans are confused and angry, we need a better way to think about what our measurements tell us, and medicine offers the right analogy. A hospital monitors pulse, temperature and blood pressure because they are cheap to measure, can be measured continuously, and correlate well with health. Yet every clinician knows that a patient can have normal vital signs and be seriously unwell, which is why the patient's own account of how they feel counts as evidence too, and why a good doctor asks.

Our systems have the same two kinds of evidence. Health checks, CPU graphs and pod counts are a system's vital signs: cheap, continuous and usually correlated with things going well. What the fan experiences in the queue and at checkout is the patient's account. Most of the time the two agree. The outages that surprise us, the ones where the on-call engineer stares at a green dashboard while the support queue fills, hide in the places where they disagree. Here the analogy breaks, and it breaks in our favor. A doctor can only ask the patient, and must interpret what the patient says; software can measure its users' experience directly, by recording each fan's request and whether it succeeded from the fan's point of view, as Chapter 4 shows. Figure 1.6 lays some of these disagreements side by side for Encore.

| What the system reports | What the fan feels |
|---|---|
| All Seat Inventory pods healthy | "Seat unavailable" on every seat I click, because one shard is mid-failover |
| Payment API 99.95% available | My card was charged and the page spun until it timed out |
| Notification delivery success 100% | I got my ticket twice and don't know which one is valid |
| Sale Gate p99 latency 40 ms | I have been in the queue 25 minutes and the position hasn't moved |
| Bot Screening blocked 2% of sessions | I am a real fan and I was told I am a robot |

*Figure 1.6 — Vital signs against the patient's account. Each left-hand statement can be true while the right-hand one is also true.*

Every row of Figure 1.6 is a case where both columns are honest. The pods really are healthy, and the fan really cannot buy a seat, because the shard holding that fan's seats is in the middle of handing leadership from one replica to another. The Gate really does answer quickly, and the fan really is stuck, because a fast answer that says the queue has not moved is no help to anyone. None of these is a measurement error; each is a measurement of the wrong thing for the question the fan is asking.

Two features of user experience come out of these rows, and they shape the rest of the book. The first is that failure is usually partial. Encore is rarely simply up or down; one shard is failing over, one payment provider is slow, one tenant's cell is unhealthy, and everyone else is fine. So the useful questions about an incident are "for whom?" and "how many?", and a measurement that averages over all fans will hide a failure that hits a tenth of them completely.

The second is that users feel their own latency, never the median. A fan does not experience Encore's typical response time; the fan experiences this request, now, and if it is slow, the fact that most requests are fast is no comfort. This matters more than it first appears, because a single purchase touches a dozen services. Suppose each of them is slow on only 1% of requests. The chance that one fan's purchase avoids every slow response is 0.99 multiplied by itself 12 times, about 89%, which means roughly one purchase in nine meets the slow tail of at least one service. A rare slowness in each part becomes a common experience for the whole, as [the Architecture book's tail-at-scale argument](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#tails-not-averages) showed, and that is why later chapters measure percentiles and individual journeys instead of averages.

**Recap.** Reliability is the probability of doing the intended job under stated conditions over a stated period. Availability is narrower: the share of time or requests the system can serve. Correctness, whether the answers are right, is a third property that availability as commonly measured cannot see. Fast recovery is usually the cheapest route to availability, so we design for it, but we do not steer by average recovery time, which is too noisy to trust. And because failure is partial and each user feels their own latency, we ask who was hurt and how many, and listen to the patient as well as the vital signs.

**Exercise 1.3.** For a user journey you own, write five rows in the style of Figure 1.6: on the left, something your system reports as healthy; on the right, something a user could be feeling at the same moment. Which of the right-hand column could you detect today, and how?

### 1.4 The Modern Production Stack

#### Layers, and why we need them {#layers-and-why-we-need-them}

A touring concert production is organized in layers. The riggers hang the structure that everything else will rest on; the power crew brings in electricity and distributes it safely; the sound and lighting desks run their own systems on top of that power; and the stage manager calls the cues that turn all of it into a show. Each layer has its own specialists, its own vocabulary and its own checklists, and each depends on the layers beneath it. The lighting designer does not need to know how the truss was rated, only that it was rated and will hold. That arrangement is what lets a few dozen people assemble, in an afternoon, something none of them could build alone.

Production software is organized the same way, and for the same reason. Hierarchy is how we master a whole that is too complex to hold in one head at once: each layer hides its detail from the layers above it, so that the people working at one level can reason about the level below through a small, stable promise instead of through everything that lies beneath. The idea of arranging operational practice as a layered hierarchy has a precedent. Google's *Site Reliability Engineering* (2016) includes a **Service Reliability Hierarchy**, drawn by Mikey Dickerson, which stacks the practices from the bottom up: monitoring, then incident response, then postmortems, then testing and release, then capacity planning, and finally development and product at the top. The ordering carries an argument, and it is worth following. A team cannot respond to a problem it cannot see, so monitoring comes first; it cannot learn from an incident it never handled, so postmortems sit above incident response; and only once those foundations are in place does it make sense to invest in the higher practices. No standard production stack exists, and I would distrust anyone who claimed to have one. The stack in this section is a teaching device, offered in that tradition, and its value lies in the questions it helps us ask, not in any claim that the world is built this way.

Let us walk it from the bottom up. At the foundation sits the **runtime and its control plane**: the clusters, functions and managed services on which our code actually executes. Those resources are declared as code and **reconciled** continuously, which is to say that automated controllers keep comparing what we declared with what is actually running and keep acting to close any difference between the two. Above the runtime, **delivery** carries change into production through pipelines, canaries and feature flags. **Telemetry** reports what the system is doing, and **SLOs** judge whether what it is doing is good enough for its users. When users are hurting, the **incident process** takes over, and once the hurting stops, **learning** decides what changes as a result. Alongside all of these runs the **platform**, which packages the lower layers into paved paths so that each of Encore's 40 teams does not have to assemble them from parts. Two concerns sit on no single layer. Security operations is one of them, because attackers use our runtime, our pipeline and our credentials as readily as we do, and a defense placed on only one layer leaves the others open. AI workloads are the other, because they run on all of the layers at once and bring their own peculiarities to each. Figure 1.7 lists the stack's rows, which is not the bottom-to-top order we have just walked, and says where each row is developed, and that makes the figure a reading map for both companion books as much as a diagram. This book takes the rows that concern the people and machines who run the system: the safety foundations that explain why complex systems fail, telemetry, incident response, learning, and the organization. The Resilience book takes the rows that concern engineering the system itself, from objectives and capacity through the runtime, delivery, resilience, data, security and AI workloads. The figure also names several layers the walk passed over, such as the capacity, resilience and data concerns that sit between telemetry and the runtime.

| Layer | The question it answers | Encore today | Where it is developed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Safety foundations | Why do complex systems fail? | The first outage, re-read | This book, Chapters 2 and 3 |
| SLOs and error budgets | Is it good enough for users? | Seat-map and tenant SLOs | Resilience book, Chapter 1 |
| Telemetry and observability | What is it doing, and why? | Traces across the purchase path | This book, Chapter 4 |
| Capacity and cost | Will it hold, and at what price? | 1,200 fans/s, four shards, cost per ticket | Resilience book, Chapter 2 |
| Runtime and control plane | Where does it run, and what keeps it there? | Kubernetes cells, serverless Gate, GitOps | Resilience book, Chapter 3 |
| Delivery | How does change arrive safely? | Canaries, flags, the on-sale freeze | Resilience book, Chapter 4 |
| Resilience | What happens when parts fail? | Retry budgets, load shedding, cells | Resilience book, Chapter 5 |
| Data and recovery | What is lost, and how fast do we return? | A seat ledger that may lose nothing | Resilience book, Chapter 6 |
| Security operations | Who else is acting on the system? | Bots, leaked keys, signed artifacts | Resilience book, Chapter 7 |
| Incident response | What do we do while users hurt? | Incident commander rotation | This book, Chapter 5 |
| Learning from incidents | What do we change afterwards? | Postmortems that change the system | This book, Chapter 6 |
| AI workloads | How do we run nondeterministic components? | Support assistant, refund agent, AI gateway | Resilience book, Chapter 8 |
| Platform and organization | Who owns what? | Golden paths, the Reliability team | Resilience book, Chapter 3; this book, Chapter 7 |

*Figure 1.7 — The production stack across the two companion books, not bottom to top; security and AI workloads cut across the other rows.*

#### What the stack hides, and what it cannot {#what-the-stack-hides}

A hierarchy earns its keep only while the interfaces between its layers behave as documented. As long as each layer keeps its promise, the layers above it can safely ignore what happens underneath. Real events, however, rarely stay inside the layer where they began. Consider a single node drain, the routine operation in which the runtime moves workloads off a machine so that the machine can be upgraded or retired. In the runtime it is housekeeping. In telemetry it shows up as a latency spike while the displaced work settles elsewhere. Against an SLO it appears as a faster burn of the error budget. To the engineer on call it arrives as a page, which may become an incident, and if we are diligent the incident ends in a change to how delivery or the runtime handles drains in the future. One event has passed through five or six layers, and at each layer it looks like a different thing.

This is where the touring analogy gives out. A tour has one stage manager calling every cue from a single script, so there is at least one person who knows the whole show and the order in which things will happen. A cloud system has no such person. Its cues are called by hundreds of control loops (autoscalers, schedulers, reconcilers, deployment controllers) and none of them is aware of all the others; each acts sensibly on its own small view of the world. No layer sees the whole of an event, and part of the reason the incident process exists is to assemble that whole after the fact, from the fragments each layer holds. Figure 1.8 shows the node drain from each layer's point of view.

{% include diagrams/fig-1-8.svg %}

*Figure 1.8 — One node drain, as each layer of the stack sees it. Every layer holds a piece of the event and none holds all of it, which is why an incident needs someone to assemble the whole.*

The largest outages of recent years were of exactly this kind. In July 2024 a content update to CrowdStrike's Falcon sensor went out to machines around the world at once and crashed millions of Windows computers. In June 2025 a policy change with blank fields reached Google Cloud's Service Control, the system that checks API requests, and crashed it in region after region. In November 2025 a bot-management file at Cloudflare doubled in size, overflowed a limit in the software that read it, and took much of Cloudflare's network down with it. The Resilience book tells all three of those stories in its chapter on shipping change safely. In October 2025 a race between two copies of AWS's own DNS automation emptied the DNS record for DynamoDB's endpoint in the us-east-1 region, setting off the fourth, which Chapter 3 of this book tells. What these four have in common matters more than their details. None of them began with a broken machine, a failed disk or a severed cable, the failures our older instincts prepare us for. Each began with a change to configuration or to automation, a change that someone or something had made deliberately, and each crossed layers faster than anyone could follow it. I have come to believe that modern production failure is mostly self-inflicted, and that its blast radius is set less by the size of the mistake than by how far a change can travel before anyone sees it. That is why so much of this book and its companion is about delivery, admission and the speed of our own automation, and comparatively little is about hardware.

The stack also has a limit that no amount of layering removes, and the Notification rewrite shows it plainly. On the morning after that rewrite, every layer had done its job. The pipeline had run its checks, the canary had compared the new version against its SLO, and the result had been judged acceptable. Yet no layer had required anyone to read the 40 files, and none of them could say whether "acceptable" had been defined correctly in the first place, because a duplicate ticket counts as a successful delivery in every measure the layers knew how to take. Catching that took something the stack does not supply: a specification of behavior, written by someone who understood what a ticket is for. Layers can enforce a judgment once a person has made it and written it down; they cannot make it for us. What a system must offer to the people who make those judgments, especially when they did not write the code, is the subject of the next section.

**Recap.** The production stack is a teaching hierarchy in the line of Google's Service Reliability Hierarchy: each layer hides detail from the ones above, and security and AI workloads cut across all of them. The hierarchy holds only while its interfaces behave, and real events cross layers that each see only a fragment. The great outages of 2024 and 2025 each began with a deliberate change and crossed the whole stack before anyone could follow it.

**Exercise 1.4.** Fill in Figure 1.7's "Encore today" column for your own system. Mark each row strong, adequate or missing, and then pick the weakest row on your most important user journey: the one where a failure would cost you most.

### 1.5 Principles for Operable Systems

#### A definition, and a stranger {#a-definition-and-a-stranger}

We have now seen, from several directions, the same uncomfortable fact: operators have never been able to understand everything they run. The operator behind the closed-shop counter could not usefully read the programs in the card decks, and Encore's on-call engineer could not have read the 40 files of the new Notification service before breakfast. If complete understanding was never available, then the useful question is what a system owes to the people who must run it without that understanding. The name for the answer is operability. **Operability** is the degree to which a system can be understood, controlled and repaired in production by people who did not build it, under time pressure and with partial knowledge.

Each part of that definition does work. "People who did not build it" is the ordinary case, because every author will one day be asleep, on holiday or gone to another company, and with coding agents the author may never have been a person at all. "Under time pressure" matters because an explanation that takes an afternoon to assemble is no help during an on-sale that lasts minutes. "Partial knowledge" admits that the operator will always be missing something, so the system has to be designed for someone who is partly in the dark. The figure to keep in mind is a capable stranger, woken at 3 a.m., looking at this system for the first time. Operability measures how much that stranger can accomplish.

None of what follows is new, and it is worth saying so, because principles that have survived several generations of technology deserve more trust than ones invented last year. James Hamilton's 2007 paper, which we met in Section 1.3, drawn from his experience running Microsoft's Windows Live services, reads as a list of obligations that a service has to its operators, gathered under three tenets: expect failures, keep things simple, automate everything. AWS later wrote much of the same advice into the operational excellence principles of its Well-Architected Framework, which ask for frequent, small, reversible changes, for anticipating failure, and for learning from every operational failure. The five principles below are this book's distillation of that tradition. They apply equally to code written by people and to code written by agents, because the stranger at 3 a.m. is in the same position either way.

**Legible.** A legible system tells us what it is doing, in terms of its users' experience, without anyone having to read its source. The emphasis on users matters. A service can report healthy processes, low latency and a 100% delivery rate while fans receive their tickets twice, as Section 1.3 showed. Legibility means the system reports the things a user would care about, in a form an operator can check. Encore's new Notification service now emits a delivery record for every ticket, carrying the order it belongs to and the attempt number. If a duplicate ever escaped, two records for the same order would appear side by side, and a simple query or alert would show it within minutes, long before the first confused fan wrote to Support.

**Steerable.** A steerable system offers controls that operators can turn safely and quickly while it runs. At Encore these include the Gate's admission rate, feature flags, the ability to drain traffic away from a node or a cell, and the thresholds at which the system starts shedding load. The best controls let the operator degrade the product rather than switch it off. Hamilton described a "big red switch" that sheds non-essential work under extreme load so that the essential work can continue; on a ticket site under strain, that might mean drawing a simpler seat map and continuing to sell tickets. There is a catch, and it is why this principle carries a condition. A control that has never been turned may not work when it is needed, and a control anyone can turn is a new way to cause an outage. So controls are tested and access-controlled like any other change to production.

**Bounded.** In a bounded system, when something fails, the damage stays inside a known perimeter: a cell, a shard, a tenant or a slice of traffic. [The Architecture book](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#blast-radius-is-a-design-variable) drew these boundaries for Encore. Here we add the operational condition that gives them their value. A boundary makes a bad change survivable only if changes cross it one at a time. If a new version reaches one cell first, its damage is confined to that cell while someone notices; if a configuration is pushed to every cell at once, the cells offer no protection at all, because the change has no perimeter. This is the lesson of the large outages in Section 1.4, each of which began with a change that travelled everywhere before anyone saw it. A bounded system should also be **statically stable**, in the Amazon Builders' Library's phrase: what is already running keeps serving when a control plane or a dependency fails. The seat ledger, for example, should keep selling seats even if the system that deploys and schedules it is down. The Resilience book develops both ideas in its chapter on designing for failure.

**Reversible.** In a reversible system, every change can be undone faster than it can do serious harm. Most changes, such as a new version of a stateless service, can be rolled back simply by restoring the previous version. Some cannot, and these are mostly changes to data: a schema migration that drops a column, or a backfill that rewrites records. Reversibility asks that we identify such changes in advance and break them into steps that can each be undone, for instance by adding a new column and copying data into it before removing the old one. The Notification canary is the plain example. Promoting a version nobody had read in full was a reasonable risk only because the previous version could be restored in minutes if the new one misbehaved.

**Rehearsed.** A recovery path that has never been exercised is untested, and an untested path should be assumed broken. Failover scripts go stale as the system changes around them, and a rollback that worked a year ago may depend on something that no longer exists. Hamilton insisted that rollback be tested and recovery scripts be exercised in production, where the real conditions are. Google's Disaster Recovery Testing (DiRT) program turned such exercises into a yearly habit. The point of rehearsal is to discover the broken step on a calm afternoon, when there is time to fix it, rather than during the incident it was meant to resolve.

#### Ownership, which holds the principles together {#ownership-holds-them-together}

The five principles describe the system, but they do not maintain themselves. Telemetry drifts, controls lose their tests and runbooks go stale unless someone is responsible for keeping them current. So the tradition insists on a sixth principle, which belongs to the organization rather than the code. Every production system needs an **owner**: a named team that answers its pages, keeps its runbooks current and decides when its risks are acceptable.

The Notification rewrite shows ownership at work. The Fan Messaging team woke that morning owning code it had not written, which is the operator's oldest position, the one the closed shop knew in the 1950s. What made that position tolerable was a combination of two things. The first was a service with the five properties: it could be observed, steered, contained, rolled back and recovered. The second was an owner who used its authority, in the form of one engineer's 20 minutes spent on the specification, which caught the duplicate-ticket risk that every automated check had passed.

A principle helps only if we can check whether a service meets it. Figure 1.9 therefore turns the six into questions that a readiness review can put to any service before launch. Each question is answered with evidence, because a confident "yes" in a meeting is not the same as a timed rollback.

| Principle | Ask before launch | Evidence that settles it |
|---|---|---|
| Legible | Can on-call tell from telemetry whether users are served correctly? | A user-journey service level indicator |
| Steerable | Which controls turn at runtime, who may turn them, and how fast? | Each control exercised, access logged |
| Bounded | How many users can one bad change or one failed dependency reach? | Per-cell rollout stages; serving survives a control-plane outage |
| Reversible | How is each change undone, and which changes cannot be? | A timed rollback; irreversible changes listed |
| Rehearsed | When were failover, restore and rollback last exercised? | Dates and durations from the last drill |
| Owned | Who answers the page, with what runbook and what authority? | A named team in the service catalog |

*Figure 1.9 — Operability as a readiness checklist. Chapter 7 returns to the review that asks these questions.*

I have come to believe that these principles answer the question with which the chapter began: who runs code that nobody fully read, and what do they need from it at 3 a.m.? We cannot read everything we run, and we never could; the operators of the 1950s could not, and neither can Encore's engineers facing 40 files written overnight by an agent. What we can do is insist that whatever we run, whoever or whatever wrote it, comes with what a stranger needs to operate it at that hour: a way to see what it is doing, controls to steer it, walls to contain it, a way back, and practice in using all of them. We can also insist that someone is accountable for all of this, which matters most on the night when the stranger turns out to be us. Operability is the modern form of the contract that the run book once carried between a program and its operators. Like every contract, it is worth only as much as its enforcement, which is why the readiness review asks for evidence and why the rest of this book is largely about how that evidence is produced.

**Recap.** Operability is the degree to which strangers can understand, control and repair a system under pressure. Five principles, distilled from Hamilton's 2007 advice and AWS's operational excellence principles, describe what such a system offers: it is legible, steerable, bounded, reversible and rehearsed. Ownership holds them together, because someone must keep each property true as the system changes. Each principle also holds only under its condition: controls that have been tested, boundaries that changes cross one at a time, and rollbacks that have been practiced.

**Exercise 1.5.** Answer Figure 1.9's questions for a service you own, with evidence, and score the service from one to five on each principle. For the lowest score, describe the situation at 3 a.m. in which that weakness would hurt, and the smallest change that would raise the score.

### Kata: The Night Ferry

> **Your brief: "Tideway."** Tideway is a ferry operator, and its booking platform sells crossings on 14 island routes, about 9,000 bookings on an ordinary day. The ordinary day is not the hard one. When a storm cancels crossings, every passenger on those sailings needs a new one at once, and rebooking traffic jumps 40× within an hour. Some of those passengers are island residents travelling to hospital appointments on the mainland, and they cannot afford to be stranded. The platform is built by 22 developers in four teams. Running it falls to a three-person infrastructure team, which receives releases on Thursday evenings and holds the only pager. Half of last quarter's code came from a contractor's coding agent, and the contract ended in September. Tideway learns of outages when port staff phone the office. The board has asked for one thing: "fewer surprises, without slowing down."

The brief is short, but nearly every sentence in it touches a section of this chapter, and the deliverables below ask you to make those connections explicit. Work through them in order, because each one builds on the picture the previous one leaves you with.

**Deliverables:**

1. **Sociotechnical map** — Draw the people, automated systems and external parties involved when a storm forces a rebooking, and note for each who knows what about it, and how well. Section 1.1 argued that reliability is produced jointly by people and machines, so the map should show where knowledge and judgment live, and where they are missing, as clearly as it shows the boxes.
2. **Operations history** — Place Tideway in Section 1.2's lineage, from the closed shop to the SRE tradition. Then find its counter, the place where code passes from the people who wrote it to the people who run it, and say what crosses that counter along with the code and what is lost in the crossing. Ask the same question of the coding agent's code, whose author is no longer there to ask.
3. **User-felt reliability** — Build a version of Figure 1.6 for storm rebooking, setting what Tideway's systems would report against what a passenger would feel. Include at least two rows in which availability looks healthy while residents are still being failed: the kind of gap that today surfaces only when a port worker picks up the phone.
4. **Stack map** — Build a version of Figure 1.7 for Tideway and rate each layer strong, adequate or missing. Then choose three layers in which to invest first, and justify each choice by what it would change on the night of a storm.
5. **Operability review** — Answer Figure 1.9's questions for the rebooking service, with the evidence you would expect to find, or the admission that none exists. Name an owner for the contractor's code: the team that will answer its pages, keep its runbooks current and decide which of its risks are acceptable. Finish with a 90-day plan.

**Rubric:**

| Criterion | The question to ask yourself |
|---|---|
| Sociotechnical view | Does the map show people and the judgment they exercise, or only boxes and arrows? |
| Honest lineage | Have you recognized the Thursday handover as a closed-shop counter, and said what is lost when releases cross it? |
| User truth | Would a resident stranded on the wrong side of the water recognize the right-hand column of your table as their own experience? |
| Prioritization | Are the layers you chose the ones a storm rebooking actually passes through, rather than the most fashionable ones? |
| Ownership | Does the contractor's code now have a named owner who can operate it without first reading all of it? |
| Restraint | Could 22 developers and three operators carry out this plan, and does it honor the board's "without slowing down"? |

The last criterion deserves a word, because an ambitious answer fails it most easily. A plan that rebuilds Tideway's operations from the ground up may be right in every particular and still be useless, since a small organization can absorb only so much change while it keeps its ferries sailing. Good answers to this kata usually look modest on paper: a handful of changes, each chosen because it closes a gap that the next storm would otherwise find.

#### Where you now stand

You can now describe production as a sociotechnical system, whose reliability its people and machines produce together. You can place your own organization in the history of running software and find the counter where its code changes hands. You can separate what a dashboard reports from what a user feels, map the layers of the production stack, and judge whether a system is operable no matter who, or what, wrote its code. What you still lack is an account of how such systems actually fail, and why that failure is so seldom the work of a single broken part. Chapter 2 turns to safety science, from Three Mile Island onward, for that account.

- Algirdas Avižienis et al. — [*Basic Concepts and Taxonomy of Dependable and Secure Computing*](https://www.inf.ed.ac.uk/teaching/courses/seoc/2006_2007/resources/BasicConcepts.pdf). IEEE Transactions on Dependable and Secure Computing, 2004 — free online.
