## Chapter 2: How Complex Systems Fail — Safety Science for Software {#ch2}

> "Complex systems run in degraded mode." — Richard Cook

At four o'clock in the morning on 28 March 1979, the pumps feeding water to the steam generators of Unit 2 at Three Mile Island, near Harrisburg, Pennsylvania, stopped. What followed was the worst accident in the history of American commercial nuclear power, and yet nothing in it was exotic. A valve stuck open, and the light in the control room that was meant to show the valve's state reported only that the command to close it had been sent. The operators were conscientious people, and they did what their training and their instruments told them to do. In doing so they starved the reactor core of the water it needed. By the time anyone understood what was really happening, about half the core had melted.

The accident is usually told as a story about operators who made mistakes, and that version is comforting, because it suggests the cure is simply better operators. Charles Perrow, a sociologist who studied organizations, told it differently. In *Normal Accidents* (1984) he argued that Three Mile Island was a property of the kind of system the plant was, rather than a failing of the people who happened to be on shift. Its parts could interact in ways nobody had anticipated, and they were bound together so tightly that there was no time to understand those interactions before they did harm. In systems built that way, Perrow said, accidents are *normal*. He did not mean frequent. He meant rare, perhaps, but inevitable: they follow from how the system is put together, so no amount of care at the controls can rule them out. That one word opened a line of thought that runs through the work of Reason, Rasmussen, Cook, Dekker, Hollnagel, Woods and Leveson, and it is the foundation on which this book rests.

It belongs in a book about running software because our systems have grown into the shape Perrow worried about. A modern purchase path is a web of services, shared connection pools, retries, autoscalers and health checks, each sensible on its own and all of them able to affect one another in ways no architecture diagram shows. So this chapter asks a plain question: why do carefully built, well-defended systems still fail, and what does that imply for the people who run them? The safety scientists offer several answers, each a different model of how failure arises, and every model here ends in something a team can actually do. Some of those practices are long proven; others are still young in software. I will say which is which as we go, because an idea borrowed from another field deserves to be judged by how well it has traveled.

Encore gives us two events to re-read in this light. The first is the retry storm that followed a slow payment provider, which we will come to see as a textbook case of tangled interactions under tight coupling. The second is a failure that did not happen: the duplicate ticket that one engineer caught in an agent-written change. It shows the other half of the story, the ordinary daily work by which people keep systems safe, which rarely gets told because nothing went wrong. (Chapter 1's [map of Encore](/prodsys-book/ch/1/#encore) shows where each piece sits.)

### 2.1 Normal Accidents — Coupling and Interactive Complexity

#### What happened at Three Mile Island {#what-happened-at-three-mile-island}

The chain of events is worth following slowly, because every link in it was reasonable. When the feedwater pumps stopped, the plant lost its way of carrying heat away from the reactor, and pressure in the reactor's cooling system began to rise. Two protective responses followed, both exactly as designed: a relief valve on the pressurizer (the vessel that keeps the cooling water under pressure) opened to let off the excess, and the reactor shut itself down. Seconds later, with pressure falling again, the control system commanded the relief valve to close. The valve did not close. For more than two hours it stood open and vented coolant into a drain tank, and the water that should have been covering the core quietly left the system.

Nobody in the control room knew this, and the reason is instructive. The indicator light for the relief valve went out, which the operators reasonably read as "the valve is closed". But the light was wired to report only that the signal to close had been sent. It described the command and said nothing about the valve itself, so the one instrument that could have revealed the leak told a comforting and false story. Meanwhile a second, unrelated fault was waiting: two valves on the backup feedwater lines had been left shut after maintenance, so the backup supply that should have taken over could not reach the steam generators at first. Amid more than a hundred alarms, the operators fixed their attention on the pressurizer level, and they watched it rise. Their training had taught them that the worst thing a pressurizer could do was fill completely with water, because a full pressurizer can no longer cushion pressure changes. So they did what their training told them and throttled back the emergency cooling that had started automatically. What they could not see was why the level was rising. Steam was forming in the overheating reactor, and the expanding bubbles were pushing water up into the pressurizer. The core was being uncovered while the instrument they trusted most said the system was flooding.

Read this way, the story has no villain. No single part broke in a way that explains the outcome, and there is no step at which a reasonable person, knowing only what the operators knew at that moment, would obviously have acted otherwise. The stuck valve, the misleading light, the closed feedwater valves and the training that warned against a full pressurizer were each small and survivable alone; what made them deadly was the way they met. The presidential commission that investigated the accident reached a correspondingly broad verdict, faulting training, procedures, control-room design and the regulator alike. It was Perrow who explained why a systemic answer was the right one, and why we should expect accidents like this in systems built like this.

#### Two dimensions of trouble {#two-dimensions-of-trouble}

Perrow's explanation rests on two questions we can ask of any system: how its parts interact, and how tightly they are bound together. Each question has two broad answers, and together they give a map.

The first question concerns interaction. In a system with **linear interactions**, parts affect each other in expected, visible sequences, like stations on an assembly line: if one station stops, the next one starves, and everyone can see why. A system has **interactive complexity** when its parts can interact in unplanned and often invisible ways, through shared components and feedback loops, so that a failure in one place appears as a symptom somewhere else. Three Mile Island was full of such interactions. A relief valve and a level gauge are not connected on any drawing, yet the open valve made the gauge lie.

The second question concerns coupling. A **tightly coupled** system has little slack: its processes run fast, cannot be paused while someone thinks, must happen in a fixed order, and have few substitutes for parts that fail. A **loosely coupled** system has buffers, spare time and alternative paths, and so it can tolerate a fault while people work out what is going on. A reactor that heats up within minutes of losing cooling is tightly coupled; a post office, where a delayed sorting machine means letters wait in a bin until morning, is loosely coupled.

Software engineers will hear an echo here. We have long used the word "coupling" to mean how much one module depends on another's internals. Perrow's sense is related but not the same. He is asking about time and slack in operation, about whether a disturbance in one part reaches another part before anyone can intervene. Two services can have beautifully separated code and still be tightly coupled in Perrow's sense, if one waits synchronously on the other with no buffer between them.

Placing the two questions on two axes gives Perrow's chart, shown in Figure 2.1 with his examples alongside some software analogues of our own.

| | Linear interactions | Complex interactions |
|---|---|---|
| **Tight coupling** | Dam, power grid · a batch payment file with a bank cutoff | Nuclear plant, chemical plant · a synchronous service mesh under peak load |
| **Loose coupling** | Assembly line, post office · a nightly report job | University, research lab · an event-driven system with durable queues |

*Figure 2.1 — Perrow's interaction and coupling chart. His examples come first in each cell; the software analogues after them are ours. Normal accidents live in the top-right cell.*

The top-right cell is where the trouble lies, and the reason is subtle: each property defeats the usual remedy for the other. In a linear system, we can prevent accidents by prediction, since we can trace the sequences in advance and guard each one. Interactive complexity takes that remedy away, because the dangerous interactions are precisely the ones nobody foresaw. When we cannot predict, we fall back on people, trusting operators to notice the surprise, understand it and act. Tight coupling takes that remedy away in turn, because it gives them no time; the disturbance has spread before anyone could understand it. A system with complex interactions but loose coupling, like a university, is full of surprises yet forgiving, because there is usually time to sort them out. A tightly coupled but linear system, like a dam, is unforgiving but predictable, so its failure modes can be designed against. Only when both properties hold together do we lose both defenses at once, and that is where Perrow's word "normal" applies. In such systems accidents are not a sign that someone was careless. They are what the system's structure makes inevitable, given enough time.

Perrow's most uncomfortable observation follows directly from this reasoning, and it concerns safety devices. Our natural response to an accident is to add protection: another alarm, another interlock, another redundant part. Yet every one of those additions is itself a component that can interact with the others, so each one raises the system's interactive complexity a little. Protection added without care can become a cause of the next accident.

History supplied a stark example well before Perrow wrote. At the Enrico Fermi 1 reactor near Detroit in 1966, a zirconium plate had been installed as a safety feature. It broke loose and blocked the flow of coolant, causing a partial meltdown. The device meant to protect the reactor was the thing that damaged it.

Software has its own safety devices that cause accidents, and they are often the automated ones we are proudest of. On 7 December 2021, AWS's us-east-1 region was impaired for hours after an automated activity to scale capacity in its internal network triggered a surge of client connections that congested the devices between networks. Automatic scaling exists to keep systems healthy under load. Here it was the trigger for an unhealthy system. I have come to believe that this pattern, a protective mechanism turning into the path of harm, is the most important thing Perrow teaches software engineers, because our systems are thick with exactly such mechanisms, and we rarely test how they behave together.

#### Encore's retry storm, re-read {#encores-retry-storm-re-read}

Encore's first big outage, told in [the Architecture book{% include xref.html to="architecture" %}](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#anatomy-of-a-self-inflicted-outage), looks different through Perrow's eyes. The facts are short. The payment service provider (PSP) slowed down, its responses taking 8 s instead of the usual 200 ms. Because each payment call now held a connection forty times longer than before, the connection pools ran dry. The health checks shared those same pools, so they could not get connections either, and they began to fail. The orchestrator, reading failed health checks as dead instances, restarted instances that were in fact healthy, merely waiting. The autoscaler read the same failures as a need for more capacity and added instances, and each new instance opened still more connections to a provider that was already struggling. Meanwhile retries at several layers of the stack each did their job independently, so a single failed purchase could become many attempts, multiplying the load on the PSP just when it could least bear it.

Now look at that list of actors. Health checks, restarts, autoscaling and retries are every one of them a safety device, added to make Encore more resilient, and each was sensible alone. The outage came from their interaction through a shared resource, the connection pool, and that interaction appeared on no architecture diagram. This is interactive complexity in its purest form, and it was made far worse by tight coupling: the purchase path called the PSP synchronously, inside a 3 s checkout budget, with no buffer and no alternative. Encore had built itself a small system in the top-right cell of Figure 2.1 without ever deciding to.

The fixes map neatly onto Perrow's two axes. The first set reduced the interactions. Retry budgets limit how much extra traffic retries may add, and keeping a single retry layer means one failure produces one bounded set of retries instead of a multiplying cascade. These are the best-proven remedies in this chapter. Google's SRE book caps retries at three per request and at about 10% of a client's traffic, and Amazon's Builders' Library advises retrying at one point in the stack. To see why the single layer matters, imagine three layers that each try a call three times: a single request at the bottom can become 27 attempts at the top, which is a load amplifier wired straight into the failure path.

The second fix loosened the coupling. Encore added a second PSP, and when the first falters, new payments move to the second. Perrow counted substitutable supplies as a mark of loose coupling, and that is exactly what a second provider is: an alternative path, so that one supplier's trouble no longer stops the whole process. For the rare case when both providers falter, Encore added slack in time as well. A verified fan's seats are held for up to 20 minutes while payment is pending, so the purchase can wait for a provider to recover rather than failing.

Perrow would have predicted what happened next, and it is worth being honest about it: the loosening brought interactions of its own. A circuit breaker, the component that decides when to switch providers, can flap, switching back and forth between them as conditions hover near its threshold. Idempotency keys, the tokens that stop a retried payment from charging a fan twice, are valid only at the provider that issued them, so a key from one PSP means nothing at the other, and a payment cannot simply be retried across the switch. And a hold that lasts 20 minutes is an attractive thing to collect; bots would happily gather pending holds to keep seats off the market, which is why the holds are capped per fan and lapse back to sale when unpaid. None of this argues against the second PSP. It argues for going in with open eyes, budgeting for the new interactions as part of the cost of the change.

What I take from Perrow is the chart as a diagnostic lens. When something goes wrong, the instinctive question is who failed. Perrow teaches us to ask a prior question: which cell was the system in? If it was in the top-right cell, the answer to "who failed" is likely to be "nobody, in particular", and the useful work lies in changing the system's structure, by reducing interactions or by adding slack.

I leave aside two parts of his work. The first is his pessimism, his conclusion that some technologies are so prone to normal accidents that they should be abandoned. The high-reliability school challenged that view with studies of organizations that operate hazardous, tightly coupled systems with remarkably few accidents, such as aircraft carriers and air traffic control, and their findings suggest that how an organization behaves matters as much as how its technology is built. The second is the chart as a precise classification method. Perrow's axes are qualitative, and nobody can measure exactly where a system sits on them, so arguing over whether a service is "really" in one cell or the next is rarely productive. The chart is most useful as a way of asking questions, not of filing answers.

In practice, that lens becomes two habits. The first is naming the couplings in every design review: for each dependency, asking how fast a disturbance in it would reach us, whether we could pause, and what our substitute would be. The second is treating every health check, autoscaler and auto-remediation as a possible cause of accidents, not merely a cure, and testing how those devices behave together through game days and fault injection, which the Resilience book{% include xref.html to="resilience" %} develops in its chapter on designing for failure, before an outage tests them for us.

**Recap.** Interactive complexity defeats prediction, and tight coupling takes away the time people need to understand a surprise as it unfolds; together they make accidents normal. Safety devices add interactions of their own, and so they can cause the very accidents they were meant to prevent. Loosening a coupling is among the most durable reliability work a team can do, provided the team budgets for the new interactions that the loosening brings.

**Exercise 2.1.** Place five components of a system you run on Figure 2.1. For the one nearest the top-right cell, name a coupling you could loosen and the new failure mode that loosening would bring. Then pick one safety device in that system, such as a health check, an autoscaler or a retry policy, whose interactions with the rest you have never tested, and sketch a game day that would test them.

### 2.2 From Human Error to System Error — Reason, Rasmussen, Cook

#### The sharp end and the blunt end {#the-sharp-end-and-the-blunt-end}

Perrow explained why certain kinds of system breed accidents. He said less about the people inside them, and for most of the twentieth century that was the part everyone thought they already understood. An accident investigation ended when it found a person. "Human error" served as both the cause and, through retraining or discipline, the cure: find the operator who turned the wrong valve, correct or replace that operator, and the system was presumed safe again. The account was attractive because it was short, and because it left the design, the budget and the schedule untouched.

James Reason, a psychologist at the University of Manchester, did more than anyone to take this account apart, and he began, interestingly, by taking error seriously rather than dismissing it. His book *Human Error* (1990) classified the ways competent people go wrong. **Slips** and **lapses** are failures in carrying out a plan that was itself sound: a slip is doing the wrong thing, as when an engineer who means to drain one node types the name of its neighbor, and a lapse is failing to do the right thing, as when the same engineer forgets to re-enable an alert after maintenance. **Mistakes** are different in kind. The plan is carried out faithfully, but the plan itself is wrong, because the person's understanding of the situation is wrong. Restarting a service that looks broken, when the real trouble is a slow dependency, is a mistake in this sense: every keystroke is correct, and the action still makes things worse. The distinction matters because the remedies differ. Slips and lapses yield to better interfaces, checklists and automation; mistakes yield only to better information and a better shared picture of what is happening.

Yet the most consequential chapter of the book looked away from the individual altogether. Reason distinguished two kinds of failure. **Active failures** are unsafe acts whose effects are felt almost at once: the slips, lapses and mistakes of the people operating the system. What he first called latent errors, and later, more precisely, **latent conditions**, are weaknesses created far away and often long before, by designers, managers and budget holders, which lie dormant until some local circumstance wakes them. A shared connection pool chosen to save memory, a health check written in a hurry, a staffing plan that leaves one person on call for three services: each of these can sit harmlessly for years. The later name is the better one, because these weaknesses are rarely errors in the ordinary sense. They are usually reasonable decisions whose cost appears only when other things go wrong at the same time.

Woods, Cook and their colleagues gave this picture its two ends. The **sharp end** is where practitioners touch the hazard: the control room, the cockpit, the on-call engineer's laptop at three in the morning. The **blunt end** is where the conditions of their work are set: budgets, schedules, tooling, staffing and policy. The words are well chosen. Force is applied at the blunt end and felt at the sharp end, and the person holding the sharp end is the one standing next to the damage when it happens, which is why that person is the easiest to see and the easiest to blame. A theater offers a fair comparison. When a cue is missed on opening night, the audience sees the stage manager who called it late; it does not see the producer who cut the technical rehearsals from three days to one, or the designer who put two cues a second apart. The comparison breaks in one useful place. In a theater everyone knows the producer exists, whereas in a software organization the blunt-end decisions are often so diffuse, spread across years of tickets and budget meetings, that nobody can say who made them. Read through this lens, operators are less the instigators of accidents than their inheritors. They receive a system already loaded with latent conditions, and on a bad night they are the ones who happen to be holding it.

Reason's most famous picture developed gradually through his writing and settled into the form we know in *Managing the Risks of Organizational Accidents* (1997). Each of a system's defenses is drawn as a slice of Swiss cheese. The holes in each slice are its weaknesses, some opened by active failures and some by latent conditions, and they are not fixed; they open, close and shift as conditions change. Most of the time a hole in one slice is covered by solid cheese in the next. An accident happens when, briefly, the holes in every slice line up and a hazard passes straight through. Reason meant it as a teaching image, and it should be held as one. Complex systems do not fail in neat straight lines through stationary slices; in Perrow's terms, the slices interact, and one defense can widen the holes in another. But the image did something more important than describe the mechanism precisely. It moved the object of study from the person at the last slice to the whole stack of defenses and the organization that built and maintained them. Figure 2.2 draws it for Encore's retry storm.

{% include diagrams/fig-2-2.svg %}

*Figure 2.2 — Reason's Swiss cheese, with the retry storm's defenses as the slices. Each hole was a latent condition set far from the sharp end; notice that every slice was a safety device, and that no single hole was enough.*

Look at what the figure says about the storm. Every slice was put there to make Encore safer: the retries to ride out transient faults, the health checks to remove sick instances, the autoscaler to add capacity under load. Each had a hole that was harmless alone. The outage needed all of them to open together, on the night the payment provider slowed down.

#### Rasmussen's boundaries {#rasmussens-boundaries}

Reason's cheese is a snapshot: it shows the holes at the moment they lined up. It says little about how the holes came to be where they were. For that we need Jens Rasmussen, the Danish engineer whose work Reason built on, and who went further in his paper "Risk Management in a Dynamic Society" (1997).

Rasmussen pictured an organization's work as a point moving within a space with three boundaries. Cross the boundary of economic failure and the organization cannot pay for itself. Cross the boundary of unacceptable workload and people cannot keep up. Cross the third, the limit of acceptable performance, and accidents happen. Inside this space the point never sits still, because two steady pressures act on it. Management, sensibly, pushes it away from economic failure and toward efficiency: do more with the same hardware, ship sooner, run leaner. The people doing the work, just as sensibly, push it away from unacceptable workload and toward least effort: take the shortcut, skip the step that never seems to matter, reuse the script that worked last time. Neither pressure is foolish, and neither is malicious. The trouble is that both gradients point the same way, toward the safety boundary, and nothing in daily work shows where that boundary is. Until the moment a team crosses it, every step toward it looked like good practice, because nothing bad happened. Figure 2.3 draws a point drifting through the space.

{% include diagrams/fig-2-3.svg %}

*Figure 2.3 — Rasmussen's migration toward the boundary of acceptable performance (after Rasmussen, 1997). Two sensible gradients push the same way, and the drift looks like noise until it arrives.*

Rasmussen's picture also answers the question Reason's left open. The holes in the cheese are where they are largely because of this migration: each slice was thinned a little by someone pursuing efficiency or saving effort, for reasons that were good at the time. This, I think, is the model to keep in mind for the rest of the book. Safety is less a state a system reaches than a position it must keep holding against steady pressure, and a team that stops pushing back will drift, through nobody's fault in particular, toward the edge. Section 2.3 follows that drift from the inside.

#### Eighteen short theses {#eighteen-short-theses}

Reason and Rasmussen wrote for researchers and safety managers. The text that carried their ideas into software engineering came from a practitioner. In 1998 Richard Cook, an anesthesiologist and safety researcher at the University of Chicago, wrote "How Complex Systems Fail", a few pages of short numbered theses that engineers have passed around more than any other text in this chapter. Part of its power lies in its form. Each thesis is a sentence or two, plain enough to read between pages, and together they turn the older research into something close to a working creed.

The core of the argument can be followed as a chain. Complex systems are hazardous, so they are heavily defended, with many layers of protection. Because they are complex, they always contain flaws (latent conditions, in Reason's language), and there are always too many to find and remove. So they run in degraded mode, with some defenses partly broken at any given moment, and they keep working because the remaining defenses and the people operating them cover the gaps. It follows that a catastrophe needs several failures together, each insufficient alone; if a single failure were enough, the defenses would already have been built to catch it. And it follows from that, in turn, that naming any one of those failures as *the* root cause misdescribes the mechanism. It satisfies our need for an explanation, and often our need for someone to blame, but it picks one strand out of a knot and calls it the knot. Cook drew one further conclusion, the most hopeful in the paper. If the system is always partly broken and still mostly works, then safety cannot be a fixed property of its design. Safety, in Cook's account, is something practitioners *do*, minute by minute, by noticing and adjusting.

The movement these three began is often called the New View, to set it against the older view that ended at the operator. Its central claim is that **human error** is an attribution made after the fact, a label investigators attach once they know the outcome, and not a cause discovered in the evidence. Hindsight makes the right action look obvious, because the investigator knows which of the many signals on the night mattered, and the operator did not. The useful question therefore shifts from who erred to why the action made sense to the person at the time, given what that person could see, knew and was under pressure to do. Woods and his co-authors call the answer to that question the second story. The first story is the one that ends at the operator. The second is the one that explains the operator, and it almost always leads back to the blunt end.

Honesty requires a qualification here, because the New View is not the whole industry's view. The example postmortem in Google's SRE book has sections for root causes and trigger, and Amazon's Correction of Error documents still walk through the 5 Whys, a technique that, followed naively, descends a single chain to a single answer. In practice, most teams that use the phrase "root cause" list several contributing factors, and the safety-science objection is less to the phrase than to stopping at one cause, above all when that cause is a person. Dropping "root cause" from the vocabulary altogether remains a minority position, held mainly by the learning-from-incidents community. A team can keep the familiar templates and still honor Cook's point, provided the review keeps going past the first answer.

#### From theory to the review room {#from-theory-to-the-review-room}

Read this way, the retry storm of Section 2.1 has no culprit. Each engineer who configured retries at a given layer followed recommended practice, since retrying a failed call is exactly what a well-behaved client is supposed to do. What nobody owned was the sum: retries at several layers multiplying one another until a slow payment provider faced a flood of traffic. The latent conditions sat at the blunt end. The health checks shared connection pools with the traffic they were meant to judge, so when the pools ran dry the health checks failed too; they measured the pools' exhaustion and reported it as the instances' sickness. The autoscaler's policy had been written for a different kind of overload, one where more instances help, and so it read the failures as a need for capacity and added instances that opened still more connections. None of these was anyone's careless act on the night. All of them were decisions, made earlier and elsewhere, that the night inherited.

Software learned to hold reviews of this kind partly from John Allspaw's 2012 Etsy post "Blameless PostMortems and a Just Culture". The title is worth reading slowly, because it joins two ideas and most people remember only the first. Blamelessness is the half that lets people describe what they did without fear, which is the only way the second story ever gets told. A just culture is the half that keeps accountability. **Just culture**, a term Reason introduced and Dekker developed, distinguishes honest error and at-risk shortcuts, which call for learning and redesign, from reckless disregard, which still calls for accountability. The distinction protects in both directions. Without it, blamelessness can sound like a promise that nothing anyone does has consequences, and people at the blunt end may hear it as permission to ignore what reviews find. With it, a team can protect the engineer whose shortcut was the normal practice of the whole team, while still acting on the rare case of someone who knowingly put others at risk.

Two habits turn all of this into practice. First, tag every contributing factor in a review as sharp end or blunt end. The tagging is quick, and it is diagnostic: a review whose findings all sit at the sharp end has stopped early, at the first story, and the second story is still waiting to be written. Second, give every blunt-end finding an owner with the authority to change it. A finding such as "health checks should not share pools with request traffic" achieves nothing if it lands with an on-call engineer who has no say over the platform's defaults. It has to reach whoever sets those defaults, or it will become one more latent condition waiting for its night.

**Recap.** Operators inherit failures whose conditions were planted far from the sharp end; sensible pressures from managers and workers alike push work toward a boundary nobody can see; and every serious failure needs several contributors at once. The lesson is never to stop at one cause, least of all at a person, while a just culture keeps accountability for genuine recklessness even as the review looks for the conditions that made ordinary actions dangerous.

**Exercise 2.2.** Take your last incident review. Mark each contributing factor as sharp end or blunt end. If the blunt-end list is shorter, the review probably stopped at the first story, so write the second story for the most prominent human action: what that person could see, knew and was under pressure to do. Then check that each blunt-end finding has an owner who can actually change it.

### 2.3 Drift and the Normalization of Deviance

#### How margins erode {#how-margins-erode}

Rasmussen gave us the picture from above: a point moving slowly through a space, pushed by two sensible gradients toward a boundary nobody can see. Sidney Dekker's *Drift into Failure* (2011) describes what that movement feels like from inside the organization that is doing the moving. **Drift** is the slow movement of a system toward failure through many small decisions, each locally reasonable and judged acceptable at the time it was made, so that afterwards no single step can be named as the moment things went wrong. Each decision is made by competent people with the information in front of them, and each, taken alone, would survive scrutiny. The danger lies in the sum, and nobody is responsible for the sum.

Drift, then, is not negligence. A negligent organization ignores its margins; a drifting one spends them, carefully and with good reasons, and is rewarded for doing so. Dekker's insight is that drift is what success looks like while it is quietly consuming its safety margins. The mechanism is self-reinforcing. Every time a team operates with a thinner margin and nothing bad happens, that uneventful outcome is read as evidence that the margin was never needed in the first place. The next decision starts from the thinner margin as its baseline, and the cycle repeats. The organization is learning, in a sense, but it is learning the wrong lesson from a sample that has not yet included the bad day.

The sociologist Diane Vaughan gave this mechanism its name: the **normalization of deviance**, the process by which a departure from a standard, repeated without consequence, comes to be accepted as the standard itself. The first exception is noticed and discussed. The fifth is routine. By the twentieth, nobody remembers that it was ever an exception. Her study of the Challenger launch decision is the home touchstone of Chapter 6, where the idea does its full work, and so we leave it there and turn instead to what drift looks like in ordinary operations.

#### The pressure that does not show on a dashboard {#the-pressure-that-does-not-show}

Encore lives in the concert world, and a touring production offers a fair analogy for how margins are spent. Suppose the load-in schedule allows four hours to rig the stage, which includes enough time to find and fix a fault if one turns up. On the first nights the crew needs most of those four hours. By the 30th night they know the rig by heart, and it goes up in three. The spare hour looks like waste, so the production manager books a press interview into it, and the venue, seeing the stage ready early, opens the doors earlier. Nobody has done anything foolish. Each of those decisions is a good one, made by someone using time that appeared to be free. Yet the hour that once existed to absorb a fault is gone, spent one good decision at a time, and the first night a truss motor fails, the show will start late.

The analogy holds up to a point, and where it fails is instructive. A touring crew can see its own clock. Everyone backstage knows how long the rig took tonight and how much time is left before doors. In software, the margin being spent is usually invisible. It might be the headroom on a database shard, which shrinks as traffic grows and nobody revisits the capacity plan. It might be the slack in a retry budget, eroded as each team adds a retry of its own. Or it might be the distance between a written rule, such as a change freeze, and what people actually do around it. None of these appears on the dashboards a team watches during an ordinary day, which is precisely why they can be spent without anyone deciding to spend them.

That last kind of margin, the gap between a rule and its practice, is easiest to see at Encore. Like many operators who face sharp peaks, Encore keeps an on-sale change freeze: no non-emergency changes to the purchase path from two hours before an on-sale until it ends. The reasoning is sound. An on-sale is the moment of greatest load and greatest consequence, and a change made then has the least time to be understood if it goes wrong. As written, the freeze is a clear margin.

As practiced, it has loosened in three ways, each with a sensible story behind it. First, exceptions are granted in chat by whichever senior engineer happens to be around. The reasons are usually good, an urgent fix or a change that seems trivially safe, but the exceptions are never recorded, so nobody can say how many there have been or who approved them. Second, platform automation considered low-risk is excluded from the freeze. This mirrors a long-standing idea in change management, where routine "standard changes" are pre-approved so that people need not seek permission for every one, and it made sense when the automation did little. The automation has since grown, and the exclusion has not been revisited. Third, the freeze covers the purchase path itself, and services that sit beside it were never covered at all. None of these three is reckless. Together they mean that the freeze protects less than the people relying on it believe.

The Reliability team, the platform group's ten site reliability engineers (SREs), has begun counting exception requests. There were three in the first quarter of last year and 14 in the most recent quarter, and none of them was followed by an incident. It would be natural to read that last fact as reassurance. Drift teaches us to read it the other way: a steadily rising number of exceptions with no visible cost is exactly the pattern in which each success makes the next exception easier to grant, and so it is what makes the trend worth attention. We should hold that reading with some humility, though. The count is Encore's own practice, not an industry measure with a known baseline, and a rising number could equally mean that the rule no longer fits the work and ought to be redesigned. Whether a freeze earns its keep at all is a question the Resilience book takes up in its chapter on shipping change safely. Here the point is narrower: whichever answer is right, Encore cannot choose between them while the exceptions live only in chat.

What can be done about drift is modest, but it is real, and it follows from the mechanism. If drift works by spending invisible margins, the first defense is to make the margins visible, so that spending one becomes a decision somebody makes and can be held to. Headroom on shards and queues can be tracked and reported. Error budgets turn a reliability target into an amount that can be seen to shrink. And exceptions to a rule belong in a register, with a named approver and a reason for each, in place of a chat thread that scrolls away. GitLab's production change lock requires exactly this kind of recorded approval, and it is a good model to borrow.

The second defense is to be suspicious of good news. A quiet period with no incidents is weak evidence that the margins are adequate, because drift produces quiet periods right up to the moment it does not. The same caution applies to aggregate incident metrics. The VOID, an open collection of published software incident reports, has analyses showing incident durations so skewed, with a few very long incidents among many short ones, that an average such as mean time to resolve (MTTR) says little about how a system is actually behaving. A falling average can hide a growing tail.

The third defense is to bring in people who were not there while the practice changed. Insiders are the last to notice drift, because they lived through each small step and each one seemed reasonable when they took it. An outsider sees only the distance between where the practice started and where it stands now. Google's production readiness review, in which SREs from outside the developing team examine a service before agreeing to take it on, is the established software form of this idea. Encore's Wednesday readiness review before each on-sale, where the Reliability team checks the capacity arithmetic and the preparations with the teams on the purchase path, has the same shape, and it is a natural place to read the exceptions register aloud.

None of these defenses stops drift. I doubt that anything does, since the pressures Rasmussen described never let up and the people responding to them are behaving sensibly. What the defenses can do is turn an invisible slide into a visible series of choices, and a choice, unlike a slide, can be questioned.

**Recap.** Drift spends safety margins through decisions that are each reasonable, and every success with a thinner margin makes the next reduction feel justified. The defenses follow from the mechanism: make margins visible, keep a register of exceptions with named approvers, treat quiet periods and averages with suspicion, and bring in reviewers who did not live through the change.

**Exercise 2.3.** Choose one production rule your team holds, such as a freeze or a review requirement, and compare it with the last three months of actual practice. List every exception you can find, who granted it and why. Then decide which should change, the rule or the practice, and write down the reason.

### 2.4 Safety-I, Safety-II, and Resilience Engineering

#### Studying what goes right {#studying-what-goes-right}

Every model in this chapter so far has been a model of failure. Perrow explained why some systems are bound to have accidents, Reason and Rasmussen explained how organizations prepare them, and Dekker explained how margins quietly drain away. All of that is valuable, and yet it leaves a strange gap. Encore's purchase path sells tickets correctly on almost every day of the year, and a study that looks only at the rare days when it does not is examining the exception and ignoring the rule. Erik Hollnagel made exactly this complaint: if a system almost always works, then studying only its failures is an odd way to understand it.

In *Safety-I and Safety-II* (2014) Hollnagel gave names to the two ways of looking. He called the traditional view **Safety-I**: safety as the condition in which as few things as possible go wrong, achieved by finding the causes of failure and removing them. Beside it he set **Safety-II**: safety as the ability to succeed under varying conditions, achieved by understanding how everyday work succeeds and then supporting it. The first view asks why things failed. The second asks why, given everything that could have gone wrong, things usually go right. Figure 2.4 sets the two side by side and places one Encore event under each.

| | Safety-I | Safety-II |
|---|---|---|
| Definition | As few things as possible go wrong | As many things as possible go right |
| What is studied | Failures, incidents, causes | Everyday work, adaptations, near misses |
| Human variability | A hazard, to be constrained | A resource, the reason things work |
| Response | Find the cause, add a barrier | Understand the adaptation, support it |
| Encore example | The retry-storm postmortem | The duplicate-ticket catch |

*Figure 2.4 — Two views of safety. Safety-II complements Safety-I; a team needs both the barrier and the understanding of why work usually succeeds.*

A word of caution belongs here before we go further. Hollnagel drew Safety-I sharply in order to make his point, and critics, Nancy Leveson among them (we meet her properly in Section 2.5), note that few practitioners ever held the caricature he described. Nobody who wrote the retry-storm postmortem believed that people were merely hazards to be fenced in. So I suggest we take the reframing and leave the polemic: Safety-II is most useful as a second lens beside the first, and the caption under the table means it when it says a team needs both.

What, then, is the core of the reframing? It is the idea of **performance variability**: the way people adjust what they do to the conditions they actually meet. No procedure anticipates every situation, so people fill the gaps, and Hollnagel's insight is that the same adjusting produces both successes and failures. An engineer who skips a step because the dashboard looks healthy and an engineer who adds a step because something feels off are doing the same kind of thing, which is adapting, and often only the outcome tells them apart afterwards. Hollnagel located resilience in the gap between **work as imagined**, the work as it is described in runbooks, procedures and pipeline definitions, and **work as done**, the work as people actually carry it out on a given day.

The theater, which gave us the stage manager of Section 2.2, offers a way to picture that gap. A stage manager's prompt book records every cue of the show as rehearsed: when the lights change, when the scenery flies in, when an actor enters. On any given night the show departs from it in small ways, because an actor holds for a laugh a beat longer or a piece of scenery sticks, and a good stage manager calls the cues to the show that is happening, not to the one in the book. The prompt book is work as imagined, and the night's performance is work as done. Here too the comparison has a limit worth naming: in a theater everyone can see the stage, whereas in software the work as done is mostly invisible, hidden in terminal sessions and private judgment, unless someone deliberately writes it down.

Section 2.3 found drift in this same gap, and both readings are right. The gap between imagined and done holds the adaptations that save us, such as the engineer who adds a step, and it also holds the shortcuts that spend our margins, such as the unrecorded freeze exception. Closing the gap entirely is neither possible nor desirable, since it would mean forbidding the adaptations along with the shortcuts. The practical task is harder and more interesting: to look into the gap, case by case, and tell the one kind from the other.

#### The catch that did not make the news {#the-catch-that-did-not-make-the-news}

Encore offers a clean example of something going right, and it deserves the same careful examination we would give an outage. Consider the agent-written rewrite of Encore's Notification service, told at the end of [the Architecture book](https://oleksiyp.github.io/arch-book/#the-inversion-the-preface-promised). A coding agent rebuilt the service overnight, with a new framework and new retry semantics, in 40 files nobody had typed or read in full.

As imagined, the safety of that change rested on the pipeline. Contracts, boundary rules and security checks were to pass, and the service level objective (SLO) canary, which sends a small slice of live traffic to the new version and checks it against the SLO before the rest follows, was to promote it. All of that happened exactly as designed. As done, something else happened as well. One engineer gave the new retry semantics 20 minutes more than the pipeline thought they needed, and in that time saw that a retry after a partial failure could send a fan the same ticket twice. None of the automated checks was looking for that, because none of them encoded the rule that, from the fan's point of view, a ticket is delivered once. The engineer wrote a spec-level idempotency test, one that states the property itself (a retried delivery never produces a second ticket), and only then merged the change.

A Safety-I organization keeps no record of this, because nothing went wrong and there is no incident to review. Safety-II asks a different question: what made the catch possible? Looked at closely, four conditions had to be present at once. There was *time*, because the change was not squeezed into a crowded release, so the engineer could spend those extra minutes without stealing them from something else. There was *context*, because the engineer had worked on ticket delivery and knew which failure mattered there. There was *authority*, because the engineer was free to slow down a pipeline that was showing green. And there was *a reason for suspicion*, because an agent had written all 40 files, and that fact alone invited a closer look.

Now consider what that list implies. A reorganization that moved the engineer away from ticket delivery would remove the context. A release calendar that packed more changes into each week would remove the time. A culture that treated a green pipeline as the final word would remove the authority. Any one of these changes would remove the catch, and nobody would notice, because a catch that does not happen leaves no trace; the first anyone would hear of it is a fan holding two tickets. I should be honest that this is reasoning from one case rather than measured evidence. It is, however, exactly the kind of reasoning Safety-II asks us to do, and the Resilience book, in its chapter on shipping change, shows Encore turning it into a review policy for machine-written change.

#### Making catches reportable {#making-catches-reportable}

If studying what goes right is worthwhile, we need a supply of material to study, and here lies the practical difficulty: catches are not reported by default. An outage announces itself with pages, dashboards and unhappy fans. A near miss is known only to the person who noticed it, and that person has every ordinary reason to say nothing, since writing it up takes time and may draw attention to how close things came.

Aviation faced the same problem and solved it long ago. NASA's Aviation Safety Reporting System, running since 1976, takes confidential near-miss reports from pilots, controllers and mechanics. It strips the identities from each report, and it gives the reporters a measure of protection from penalty. The design works for two reasons, and both are needed. It is non-punitive, so reporting costs the reporter little. And someone acts on the reports, so reporters can see that their effort changes something. A system that is safe to report into but never acts would soon go quiet, and one that acts but punishes would hear very little in the first place.

Software has nothing comparable. In our field a near miss reaches a review only if someone chooses to volunteer it, and few do: fewer than one in a hundred of the public incident reports collected by the VOID concern a near miss. The figure is not surprising once we remember that public reports are written about events customers felt, but it means the material Safety-II needs is almost entirely missing from the record we learn from.

The cheapest way to start is a short write-up, about ten minutes' work, under the same blameless rules as an incident review. The rules matter as much as the form, for the reason aviation discovered: people do not report a near miss that might be held against them. Encore's Reliability team adopted the following template after the Notification catch, and its fields follow directly from the analysis above:

```text
NEAR MISS: <what almost happened, in one line>
Noticed by / cue:      who noticed, and what they saw that others did not
What made it possible: time, context, authority, reason for suspicion
What would erode it:   deadline, reorg or tool change that removes the catch
Guard added:           test, check or limit, with an owner and a date
Read where:            the review or channel where the next person will see it
Use:                   learning only; never cited in performance reviews
```

Each line has a purpose. "What made it possible" records the four conditions, so that the organization learns what it must protect. "What would erode it" names the changes that could silently remove the catch next time, which is a warning a reorganization or a new tool would otherwise never receive. "Guard added" turns the lesson into something durable, with an owner and a date so it does not dissolve into good intentions. "Read where" answers the second half of aviation's lesson, that a report nobody reads changes nothing. And the last line is the non-punitive promise, written into the form itself so that nobody has to wonder about it.

We should be candid about how much is known here, because the practice is young in software. The learning-from-incidents movement, carried by the VOID, the Howie guide from Jeli (now PagerDuty) and the second edition of Google's SRE book (2026), is growing fast. Yet in healthcare, the field where Safety-II tools have been tried longest, reviews of those tools find little measured evidence of effect. That does not show the tools fail; it shows that nobody has yet demonstrated that they work. Confidential reporting, by contrast, is proven in aviation, and a ten-minute template costs very little. In my judgment, a proven mechanism at so small a price is reason enough to start, provided we keep an honest eye on whether it is doing any good.

#### Resilience as a capacity {#resilience-as-a-capacity}

Safety-II did not arrive alone. It belongs to a broader field, resilience engineering, which gathered around a 2004 symposium in Söderköping, Sweden, and the book that grew from that work, *Resilience Engineering: Concepts and Precepts* (2006). The field's central term deserves a careful definition, because in everyday engineering talk "resilient" often means little more than "redundant". **Resilience** is a system's capacity to adjust its functioning before, during and after disturbances, so that it sustains operations under expected and unexpected conditions alike. The weight of that definition falls on two words, *adjust* and *unexpected*. Redundancy protects against the failures we anticipated; resilience is what lets a system cope with the ones we did not.

David Woods sharpened the idea with a further term. **Graceful extensibility** is a system's ability to stretch past the envelope it was designed for, the range of load, failure and circumstance its builders planned around, when surprise pushes it there. Systems that lack it are **brittle**: they work well inside the envelope and fail abruptly at its edge. At Encore the people are often the source of that extensibility. When the automation reaches the limit of what it was built to judge, as the pipeline did with the Notification rewrite, it is an engineer's judgment that stretches the system a little further.

These ideas reached software in the early 2010s, through John Allspaw's work at Etsy and Richard Cook's 2012 Velocity talk, and they were set out most fully for software in the STELLA report (2017) of the SNAFUcatchers workshop. The report's most useful contribution is a picture. It draws a line through the system, the line of representation. Below the line sit the code, the infrastructure and the tooling: the running system itself. Above it sit the people, each holding a partial mental model of what lies below. Nobody sees below the line directly. People see the running system only through representations, such as dashboards, alerts, traces and deploy tools, and they act on it only through those same tools.

Seen this way, incidents are the moments when those models prove wrong, when the difference between someone's picture of the system and the system itself suddenly matters. The Notification catch fits the picture from the other side. The pipeline's green checks were a representation, and they showed the change as safe. The engineer's own model of ticket delivery, built from experience, disagreed with that representation, and the engineer trusted the model enough to look further. Figure 2.5 draws Encore through the line: the on-call engineer, the On-Sale team, the Reliability team and the incident commander above it, each with a different partial model; the services, clusters and payment provider below it; and between them the representations through which everyone sees and acts.

{% include diagrams/fig-2-5.svg %}

*Figure 2.5 — Above and below the line of representation, after the STELLA report (2017). Every person sees and acts on the running system through representations, and holds a partial model of what lies beneath them.*

**Recap.** Safety-I removes the causes of what goes wrong; Safety-II studies and supports the adaptations that make things go right, and a team needs both. The gap between work as imagined and work as done holds both the adaptations that save us and the drift that spends our margins, and the task is to tell them apart. Near-miss reporting is proven where it is confidential and acted on, while Safety-II programs in software are still young. Resilience is a capacity to adjust, and at Encore much of it lives in people who see the running system only through representations.

**Exercise 2.4.** Find one occasion last quarter when someone prevented a problem that no process would have caught. Write it up with the near-miss template, paying particular attention to what made the catch possible and what could erode it, and decide where the next person will read it.

### 2.5 Systems Thinking and Control — STAMP for Software

#### Safety as a control problem {#safety-as-a-control-problem}

The models we have met so far are better at explaining failure than at telling an engineer what to build. Perrow tells us which systems are dangerous, Reason tells us how defenses decay, Rasmussen tells us why work drifts toward the edge, and the resilience engineers tell us how people keep things running in spite of all that. Each is illuminating after the fact. None of them hands a team a method it can sit down with on a Tuesday afternoon and apply to a design. Nancy Leveson of MIT built a model that does both: it explains accidents, and it prescribes engineering. She set out **STAMP** (System-Theoretic Accident Model and Processes) in a 2004 paper and developed it at length in *Engineering a Safer World* (2011).

Her starting point is a distinction that sounds obvious once said and is ignored remarkably often. Reliability is a property of a component: does this part do what it was built to do? **Safety**, in STAMP, is an emergent property of the whole system, something that arises from how the parts interact and cannot be found by inspecting any one of them. The two are distinct, and the gap between them matters most in software, where many accidents happen with every component doing exactly what it was built to do. Encore's retry storm is a case in point. The retries retried, the health checks checked, and the autoscaler scaled, each faithfully to its specification, and the purchase path still fell over. A model that searches for the broken part will find nothing to fix, because nothing was broken.

What STAMP offers instead is to treat safety as a control problem. The idea has an old lineage: it belongs to the systems thinking of the mid-twentieth century, to cybernetics and control theory, in which a system stays within safe bounds because something is watching it and steering it, the way a thermostat holds a room's temperature. The model rests on three ideas.

The first is the **safety constraint**: a condition that must always hold for the system to be safe. "No seat is sold twice" is one at Encore. A safety constraint is a statement about the system's behavior, and it says nothing yet about which part enforces it.

The second is the **control structure**: a hierarchy of controllers that together enforce the constraints. Controllers may be people, organizations or software. Each one issues **control actions** downward to the thing it controls and receives **feedback** upward about what happened. A team sets a configuration, a service obeys it, and dashboards report back how it went; a regulator sets rules, a company follows them, and audits report back. The hierarchy matters because a controller high up, such as a team setting policy, acts slowly and on summaries, while one low down, such as an autoscaler, acts quickly and on raw signals.

The third, and to my mind the most useful, is the **process model**: a controller's belief about the current state of the thing it controls. No controller sees reality directly. A human operator sees instruments; an autoscaler sees metrics; a team sees reports and dashboards. Every control action is chosen on the basis of that belief, so when the belief is wrong, a perfectly sensible decision becomes a dangerous one.

Three Mile Island shows how this works. The light on the operators' panel reported that the command to close the relief valve had been sent, and the operators took it to mean the valve was closed. It was stuck open. From that moment their process model was corrupted: they believed the system held more water than it did. Every control action that followed was rational given what they believed, and wrong given what was true. Seen through STAMP, the accident was neither a broken valve nor careless operators; it was a control loop in which the feedback no longer described the process being controlled.

From this model Leveson built two methods, one for looking ahead and one for looking back. The forward-looking one is a hazard analysis method, **STPA** (System-Theoretic Process Analysis). Its central move is to go to each controller in the structure and ask how its control actions could be unsafe. STPA gives four ways, and the list is short enough to carry in one's head:

- a needed control action is **not provided**;
- a control action **is provided that causes a hazard**;
- a control action comes **too early, too late or out of order**;
- a control action is **stopped too soon or applied too long**.

The value of the four categories is that they turn a vague worry ("could the autoscaler cause trouble?") into four specific questions, each of which has an answer that can be written down and argued about. The backward-looking method is CAST (Causal Analysis based on STAMP), which applies the same structure after an accident: rather than asking which part failed, it asks which controllers had inadequate process models, which feedback was missing, and why each control action seemed reasonable at the time. Chapter 6 puts CAST to work.

#### A method arriving in software {#a-method-arriving-in-software}

Where STPA stands depends very much on the field, and the difference deserves to be stated plainly. In aerospace and automotive work it is long proven. In software operations it is arriving now, and the evidence for it there is the evidence of early adoption.

That early adoption has a serious precedent. Google's SRE organization has been applying STAMP to its own systems, as Tim Falzone and Ben Treynor Sloss described in *;login:* in December 2024. The second edition of the SRE book (O'Reilly, 2026) has a chapter on STPA and CAST, and Google engineers presented a CAST analysis of a Google Maps incident at SREcon in 2026. When the organization that gave the industry the term SRE invests in a method, it deserves attention. But Google describes the work, in its own words, as something it is still "exploring", and it has published no outcome data. We know Google finds STAMP worth doing; we do not yet know, from published evidence, what it changed. That is the honest state of the method in our field, and it is a reasonable basis for trying it carefully rather than adopting it wholesale.

There is also a practical cost. Full STPA is labor-intensive: done properly across a whole system, it means drawing every controller, every control action and every feedback path, and then working through four categories of unsafe action for each one. Few teams have the time to do that for everything they run. The sensible course is to scope the analysis to one critical path, the sequence of services and decisions whose failure would hurt most, and do it well there.

Software, as it happens, suits the method unusually well in one respect. Where its controllers are automated, their process models are written down, because they are code. An autoscaler's belief about the world is exactly the set of metrics it reads and the policy it applies to them, and anyone can open the configuration and see it. In a chemical plant, by contrast, an analyst has to reconstruct what an operator believed from interviews and logs. Human and organizational controllers are another matter: a team's belief about how much load a service can take is no more explicit in software than in any other industry, and lives in people's heads, old load-test reports and chat threads. Those controllers are often where the analysis pays off most, precisely because nobody has ever had to write their process models down.

To see what this looks like in practice, here is part of Encore's control structure around an on-sale, the moment when tickets for a big show go on sale and fans arrive in their tens of thousands:

| Controller | Controls | Process model (what it believes) | Feedback it receives |
|---|---|---|---|
| On-Sale team | Gate admission rate | Seat Inventory's safe throughput, from load tests | Load-test reports, on-sale dashboards |
| Sale Gate | Fans admitted per second | A configured rate, about 1,200 fans/s per event | Queue depth, its own error rate |
| Bot Screening | Which sessions are blocked | What bot traffic looks like, from past patterns | Block rates, appeals |
| Kubernetes autoscaler | Pod counts per service | CPU and request load per pod | Metrics, a few seconds late |
| Platform node-upgrade reconciler | Node versions in each cluster | Node health and disruption budgets | Node and pod readiness |
| Reliability team | Readiness review, freeze policy | The on-sale calendar, past incidents | Reviews, postmortems, chat |

*Figure 2.6 — Part of Encore's control structure around an on-sale. Each controller acts rationally on its own process model; hazards arise where a model is stale, incomplete or blind to another controller's actions.*

Notice how mixed the table is. Two of the controllers are teams of people and four are pieces of software, and the table treats them all alike: each controls something, each believes something, and each learns about the results through some channel. That evenhandedness is the point. A conventional architecture review would look at the Sale Gate and the autoscaler as components and at the teams as an org chart, and would never put them in the same table.

Reading the table column by column, rather than row by row, raises questions that no review of a single component would ask. The "Controls" column invites us to look for two controllers acting on the same thing without knowing of each other: the autoscaler and the node-upgrade reconciler, for instance, both change what is running in a cluster, and neither's process model includes the other. The "Process model" column invites us to ask which beliefs come from tests, and how closely those tests resemble a real on-sale; the On-Sale team's sense of Seat Inventory's safe throughput is only as good as the load test behind it. And reading the table as a whole, we can ask which controllers know that an on-sale is happening at all, and which have no way to find out. The reconciler's process model contains node health and disruption budgets, and nothing about the calendar. None of these is a defect in any single row. Each is a gap between rows, which is exactly where STAMP tells us to look.

The table is the map; the worksheet is where the analysis is done. To show it, we can apply STPA after the fact to the autoscaler from the retry storm. Recall the story from Section 2.1: the PSP slowed from 200 ms to 8 s, connection pools ran dry, health checks sharing those pools failed, and the autoscaler, reading the failures as a need for capacity, added instances that opened still more connections to an already struggling provider. Worked through the four categories, the analysis yields a page like this, and Encore's Reliability team now keeps one for each automated controller on the purchase path:

```yaml
controller: orders-payments autoscaler
constraint: "Checkout never opens more PSP connections than the PSP can serve"
process_model: "Load is CPU and requests per pod; more pods mean more capacity"
feedback_gap: "Sees its own pods, never the PSP's latency"
unsafe_control_actions:
  not_provided: "No scale-up while fans genuinely saturate the pods"
  causes_hazard: "Scale-up while the PSP is slow: each pod opens a fresh pool"
  wrong_timing: "Scale-up on metrics seconds stale, mid-restart"
  too_long: "Scale-up held for the whole slowdown, never backing off"
```

Each line of the worksheet does a different job. The constraint states what must stay true, in terms of the system rather than the autoscaler. The process model states, plainly, what the autoscaler believes; written out like this, its flaw becomes visible, because "more pods mean more capacity" is true only when the bottleneck is inside the pods. The feedback gap names what the controller cannot see. And the four unsafe actions show that the same controller can be dangerous in opposite directions: doing too little when fans really are saturating the pods is a hazard, and so is doing too much when they are not.

The second unsafe action, scaling up while the PSP is slow, is the outage itself. And the feedback gap explains why the autoscaler could not have seen it coming. Its process model held that more pods meant more capacity, and its feedback, drawn only from its own pods, never contradicted that belief, because the PSP's latency was not something it measured. The autoscaler did exactly what it was built to do on the basis of everything it could see. That is the STAMP account of the retry storm in one sentence, and it points to a fix of a different kind from "tune the autoscaler": either give the controller the feedback it lacks, or constrain the action it should not take.

#### Mastering complexity with structure {#mastering-complexity-with-structure}

There is something reassuring about where STAMP leads. Faced with systems too complex for anyone to hold in mind, it reaches for the oldest tools we have for mastering complexity: decomposition and hierarchy. Rather than trying to understand the whole system at once, it breaks the system into controllers, arranges them in a hierarchy, and asks a small, fixed set of questions of each. Software engineers have been using these same tools to tame complex designs for half a century. STAMP applies them to the operation of a system as well as to its construction.

It also brings us back to this book's through-line, that production is a sociotechnical system. In STAMP's terms, reliability is the product of a control structure in which people and machines keep each other's process models accurate. It is not achieved once and kept; it is maintained continuously, and it decays whenever a controller acts on a belief that no longer matches the system. I have come to believe that this is the most practical way to describe what an operations team actually does all day: it keeps beliefs current, its own and those of the automation it has built.

Seen that way, the chapters that follow, in this book and in the Resilience book, are all ways to keep those beliefs current. SLOs and observability are feedback, the channels through which controllers learn what the system is really doing. Delivery practices such as canaries and feature flags are safer control actions, changes made in ways that limit the harm a wrong belief can do. Incident response is control under pressure, when process models are known to be wrong and must be rebuilt quickly. And learning from incidents is the repair of the process models themselves, so that the next decision is made on a truer picture than the last.

**Recap.** STAMP treats safety as a property of the whole system, enforced by a hierarchy of controllers, each acting on a process model that may be stale, incomplete or blind to the other controllers. STPA, its forward-looking analysis, asks of each controller how its actions could be unsafe in four ways. It is proven in aerospace and young in software operations, so a control-structure table for one critical path, and a worksheet for each automated controller on it, are a modest and useful place to start.

**Exercise 2.5.** Draw a control-structure table like Figure 2.6 for one critical path in your system, including at least one automated controller and one team. For each controller, write one unsafe control action from each of STPA's four categories, and circle the one you would least like to meet in production.

### Kata: The Twice-Monthly Run

> **Your brief: "Payday."** Payday is a payroll SaaS used by 3,000 employers in four EU countries. Twice a month it computes about 600,000 payslips and sends payment files to 11 partner banks, and each bank has a hard cutoff around 16:00 local time: a file that arrives late means people are not paid on time. Its 24 engineers run three main parts: a modular monolith; a calculation engine that calls a third-party tax-rules service synchronously, so that every payslip waits on that service's answer; and a file-generation pipeline that turns the results into bank files. Last year they added auto-remediation that restarts calculation workers when they get stuck. Two months ago a configuration change nearly sent a bank a duplicate file. Nothing in the pipeline stopped it; an operations analyst noticed that the totals were twice their usual size and halted the run. The freeze before payday used to last 48 hours. It has since shrunk to "no risky changes on payday", and nobody can say who decides what is risky.

**Deliverables:**

1. **Coupling and complexity map.** Place Payday's main components on Figure 2.1, Perrow's chart of interaction against coupling, so you can see which parts sit near the top-right cell where normal accidents live. Then choose two couplings to loosen before the next run. For each, state what the loosening costs and which new failure modes it brings, because Section 2.1 taught us that every loosening, like every safety device, adds interactions of its own.
2. **Latent-condition inventory.** List at least six latent conditions, the holes planted in advance that could line up with an ordinary slip on payday, in the sense of Reason's model from Section 2.2. Tag each as sharp end (close to the person acting in the moment) or blunt end (set by design, budgets, schedules or policy). Give every blunt-end condition an owner who can actually change it, since a blunt-end finding with no owner is only a complaint.
3. **The near miss as Safety-II.** The analyst's catch is exactly the kind of event that leaves no trace when it goes well. Write it up with the near-miss template from Section 2.4: what the analyst saw, what made the doubled totals stand out, and what the analyst did next. Then decide where such reports will be filed and who will read them, so that the next catch is reported and learned from instead of forgotten.
4. **Drift register.** Set the freeze as written beside the freeze as practiced, and trace how 48 hours became "no risky changes on payday". Build a register of exceptions in which each exception has a named approver, which turns the vague question of "what is risky" into a record someone answers for. Finish with three indicators that would tell you the drift is continuing, in the spirit of Section 2.3's visible margins.
5. **Control structure.** Draw the controllers around file generation, people and automation alike, in the manner of Figure 2.6, with what each one believes and what feedback it receives. Write an STPA worksheet for the auto-remediation, treating it as a controller with a process model that may be wrong. Then design one game day that tests how the auto-remediation interacts with the tax-rules dependency: for example, what the restarts do when the calculation workers are stuck only because they are waiting on that service.

**Rubric:**

| Criterion | The question to ask yourself |
|---|---|
| Systemic framing | Does any deliverable stop at a person, or does each one reach the conditions behind the action? |
| Coupling realism | Do the loosenings respect the bank cutoffs, and do you show the arithmetic of how much time each one buys? |
| Safety devices | Is the auto-remediation analyzed and tested as a possible source of accidents, and not only as a protection? |
| Learning from success | Would the near-miss write-up help the next analyst notice, and is it safe to report? |
| Drift visibility | Could an outsider read the drift register and see the margin being spent? |
| Process models | Is there at least one controller whose model of the world is plainly stale or incomplete? |

#### Where you now stand

You can now read a system the way safety science reads it, and act on what you see. You can test how safety devices interact instead of trusting each on its own; run reviews that reach past the sharp end to the blunt end where most conditions are set; make spent margins visible before they run out; give near misses a safe place to be reported and read; and draw the control structure of a critical path, with each controller's beliefs written down where others can question them.

The first place these ideas must be applied is automation. Every script, reconciler and assistant we add plays two roles at once. It is a safety device, added to catch or correct a fault, and so, as Perrow warned, it brings new interactions. It is also a new controller, acting on a process model of its own that can go stale like anyone else's, as Leveson's work showed. Automation therefore promises to take load off people while quietly changing the work they are left with. Chapter 3 takes up that paradox, beginning with Lisanne Bainbridge's ironies of automation.
